Title: What can composition and transport explain about scaling laws?
Author: Michael Darius Eastwood

   What can composition and transport explain about scaling
                           laws?
                                            Theory · Framework paper
      Recursive Dynamics · The ARC Theory · Law I, the ARC Principle, a law under test

                                             Michael Darius Eastwood
                              Independent Researcher, London, United Kingdom
                      ORCID 0009-0004-3222-7442 michael@michaeldariuseastwood.com



                                                         Abstract
           Predicting how a system scales can help us decide whether resources and correction can keep pace
       with its growth. This framework paper asks which composition and transport assumptions justify a
       scaling prediction before the response is observed. Four classical continuous homomorphism problems
       constrain functional families; they do not classify every physical law. A global logarithmic-defect
       stability result and a regular-variation limit connect exact equations to specified approximate and
       asymptotic cases. For a declared steady-flow network, transport accounting gives 𝐶 = 𝐵ℓ¯𝑞 . Demand
       scaling 𝐵 ∼ 𝐿 𝜇 and mean-path scaling ℓ¯𝑞 ∼ 𝐿 𝜁 then give an asymptotic throughput-cost exponent
       𝜇/(𝜇 + 𝜁). The familiar 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) requires further assumptions, and inventory and body mass
       require separate bridges. Biological measurements are compared on their own variables; fungal
       factors measured on different variables are no longer pooled. The Friedmann reparametrisation
       remains an identity, with acceleration distinguished from exponential expansion. Fixed rules can
       generate superlinear trajectories, so neither physical dimension nor an architectural label fixes an
       AI capability exponent. The contribution is a conditional synthesis and a prospective transport test
       with independently measured premises, held-out predictions and serious rivals. Existing prediction
       histories, corrections and qualified companion results are retained. No universal physical ceiling,
       alignment guarantee or new empirical validation is established.

Version 3.14, revised 1 November 2026, corrects the scope of the composition, transport, biological and cosmological
claims. Earlier editions and prediction records remain intact; Appendix A records the substantive corrections.

                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


1     Introduction
I believe advanced AI is an existential risk unless values and correction are built into how it reasons; only
proof will move the world. People should retain oversight, containment and shutdown for as long as they
physically can. The research question here is one part of that programme: can information about how
gains combine predict later scaling, before a larger system is built or relied on?
    A practical example is a supply network. Increasing its size can increase both the demand served
and the distance resources travel. Predicting throughput from inventory requires knowing which of those
quantities changes, how routes are chosen and whether transport speed changes. A three-dimensional
embedding alone does not answer those questions. The same discipline applies to an AI experiment:
capability, recursive depth, inference cost and correction burden are distinct observables.
    This paper is a theoretical and methodological synthesis across mathematics, transport, biology and
computation. It distinguishes three steps: an equation constrains a family of functions; a mechanism
supplies the assumptions of that equation; and measurements test whether the mechanism predicts
observations. The first step can be a theorem while the later steps remain unresolved. A repeated
numerical exponent alone does not join them.
    The contributions are:
      • A precise account of four classical composition identities, their domains and regularity, with scoped
        stability and asymptotic extensions.
      • An explicit transport calculation showing which demand and path assumptions yield 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1),
        and which extra assumptions connect transport cost to inventory or body mass.
      • Source-specific biological and physical comparisons that separate measurements from coordinate
        identities and from historical unsupported inferences.
      • One prospective test in which independently measured premises predict held-out transport behaviour.
        The earlier numbered predictions and the full neural-scaling withdrawal remain part of the record.
    The mathematics supplies conditional results, not a universal physical classification or a numerical
safety guarantee. Sections 4 and 6 establish the formal tools; Sections 7 to 11 examine their scope through
examples. Sections 12 and 14 state the evidence and the next test. The appendices preserve the detailed
provenance, programme placement and superseded argument.


2     Related work and the contribution
A scaling explanation must do more than reproduce a familiar fraction. It must identify the quantities
being related, the rule they obey, the assumptions that select an exponent and the measurement that could
show the explanation is wrong. Within Recursive Dynamics, this paper develops those questions for
Law I, the ARC Principle, a law under test. It connects three levels of argument: a composition equation
that constrains a functional form, a specified transport model that supplies an exponent, and a prospective
test of whether that model applies. Earlier work establishes much of the mathematics and several of the
domain models. Their differences are part of the explanation.

2.1     Earlier work
Composition and scaling limits. The continuous Cauchy equations give the four homomorphism
problems stated in Section 4 (Cauchy 1821). The chosen input and output operations, their domains and
regularity conditions do the work. In the multiplicative case the result is a power-law family; it does
not choose the exponent. The connection between rescaling and power laws is established mathematics:


                                                      5
                             The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


Newman (2005, section III.E) discusses it for scale-free distributions, whose inference problem is distinct
from the regression of one physical quantity against another.
    Approximate composition and asymptotic scaling also have established theories. Hyers (1941) gives
stability for an additive equation under a global defect bound. Section 4 states and proves the real-valued
consequence needed here, with its logarithmic error metric and its limits for finite observations. Regular
variation describes positive functions whose scale ratios converge to a power, allowing a slowly varying
factor at finite scale (Bingham et al. 1987). These are mathematical results with premises to check, not
evidence that every observed scaling relationship meets them.
    Ostaszewski (2016) explicitly connects functional equations, homomorphisms, generalised group
operations and regular variation. The author’s version 2 preprint is dated 7 November 2014; the article
was published online on 20 June 2015 and in the 2016 journal volume. This prior connection is part of
the foundation of the present paper. The comparison here uses the scope stated in that work’s opening
discussion; it does not import its later technical theorems as results about physical or computational
systems.

Branching and transport models. The expression 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) is earlier work’s. West, Brown and
Enquist (1997) derive three-quarter scaling for metabolic rates from transport through space-filling
fractal networks of branching tubes, with energy dissipation minimised. They state the generalisation to
𝐷/(𝐷 + 1) when the network fills a 𝐷-dimensional volume. West, Brown and Enquist (1999) obtain
the same form in 𝐷 dimensions from a more general treatment of hierarchical-network geometry rather
than hydrodynamics. The models have stated limits: the 1997 treatment predicts small deviations in the
smallest mammals, and the 1999 treatment does not expect its relationships to apply to filamentous algae
and fungi. A fungal application therefore needs its own premises.
     Banavar, Maritan and Rinaldo (1999) derive a relationship between size and flow in networks with
local connectivity. In the most efficient class they consider, metabolic rate scales as mass to the power
𝐷/(𝐷 + 1). Their bound concerns the nutrient-delivery rate and the volume of blood in the network; it is
not a bound on every observable of a physical system. They also compare their relation with river-drainage
observations in 𝐷 = 2 and identify other distributed transport systems to which their reasoning may apply.
Banavar and co-authors (2002) develop the resource-distribution account further and explicitly work
through the 𝐷 = 1 case. Neither that case nor the fractions 1/2, 2/3 and 3/4 are new predictions of the
present paper.
     The velocity assumption matters even within a three-dimensional supply model. In the two models of
Banavar et al. (2010), constant flow velocity across body sizes gives a metabolic exponent of 2/3; allowing
velocity to scale with mass to the power 1/12, its maximum in those models, gives 3/4. Their geometric,
steady-supply and transport assumptions remain part of the result. Three-dimensional embedding alone
therefore does not select one metabolic exponent. Section 6 keeps flow-weighted transport cost, inventory
in transit and body mass separate so that the corresponding assumptions can be seen.

Other mechanisms and other meanings of dimension. Demetrius (2003, 2006) and Demetrius and
Tuszynski (2010) develop a quantum-statistical account using coupled energy-transducing oscillators and
the Debye model. The 2010 paper derives 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) in the regime it calls quantum, with 𝑑 the dimension
of the physical space in which its oscillator network is embedded, and states the values 1/2, 2/3 and
3/4 for 𝑑 = 1, 2 and 3. Its classical regime is isometric. These are different physical assumptions from
vascular branching. The flow-accounting calculation in Section 6 does not establish their equivalence or
turn their shared expression into separate empirical confirmations. Agutter and Tuszynski (2011) review
the competing explanations.
    In Bettencourt (2013), urban land area scales with population to the power 𝐷/(𝐷 + 𝐻), where 𝐷


                                                     6
                             The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


is the embedding dimension and 𝐻 is the fractal dimension of travel paths. The case 𝐷 = 2, 𝐻 = 1
gives 2/3; the expression becomes 𝐷/(𝐷 + 1) only when 𝐻 = 1. The same framework gives superlinear
socioeconomic rates, with exponent 7/6 in that case. Land area, socioeconomic output and metabolic rate
are distinct observables. Their exponents cannot be exchanged without the corresponding model and
measurement definitions.
     Zhao (2022) derives an 𝑛/(𝑛 + 1) scaling law for an 𝑛-dimensional system from producer-consumer
dynamics. That account places biomass production, eukaryote growth, mammalian brain size and the
distribution of public facilities in cities within one proposed universality class, and places linear and
superlinear scaling outside that class. Its domain statement belongs to that model; it does not establish
that every hierarchical network falls within it.
     He and Zhang (2004) derive a 4/5 law for human-brain allometry from the fractal dimension of a
brain cell’s contour. This is a different geometrical quantity from the transport-network dimension in
Section 6. An equal algebraic form does not identify the two quantities or establish the same mechanism.

Finite size and empirical limits. Maino et al. (2014) derive, from Dynamic Energy Budget theory, an
equation relating metabolic rate to mass that is the same as the finite-size form of the West, Brown and
Enquist model. It approaches the three-quarter power only at large mass. They stress that metabolic rate
is not a power function of mass under either framework. The distinction between a finite-range curve and
an asymptotic exponent is therefore substantive, not a plotting detail.
    The West, Brown and Enquist derivation has been questioned (Dodds et al. 2001; Kozłowski and
Konarzewski 2004), and Savage et al. (2008) show that its three-quarter exponent holds only in the limit
of infinite network size. Empirical comparisons also require the competing evidence: White and Seymour
(2003) report mammalian basal-metabolic estimates compatible with 2/3 under their stated adjustments
and exclusions, while Kolokotrones et al. (2010) find curvature on logarithmic axes. Section 12 sets
out the relevant measurements and their limits. A shared fraction across models is not a substitute for
choosing between their premises against evidence.

2.2   What this paper adds
The contribution is a mathematical and methodological synthesis with an explicit route from an observable’s
composition rule to a prediction that can fail. The classical functional equations, their stability theory,
regular variation and the earlier transport models supply the starting points. The paper brings them
together in three steps:
   1. Declare the equation for the observable. The input and output operations, variable pair,
      normalisation and regularity conditions are stated before a family is assigned. Exact, approximate
      and asymptotic composition are kept distinct. A saturation model supplies additional dynamics;
      boundedness alone does not put it in the Cauchy grid (Sections 3 to 5).
   2. Expose the assumptions selecting the exponent. The flow-accounting identity and conditional
      model of Section 6 state how demand and route length scale. The expression 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) is recovered
      under particular choices of those exponents. Velocity and the bridge from transport cost to biological
      mass are separate premises. Section 10 states the geometric bound only for the observable pair and
      assumptions that support it.
   3. Test the non-definitional premises and the resulting prediction. The prospective transport test in
      Section 14 separates calibration of the proposed mechanism from held-out response measurements.
      The source comparisons ask whether the declared premises apply. The cosmological example
      distinguishes a change of variables from a physical explanation (Section 9); the recursion model
      keeps an assumed gain equation separate from a measured capability trajectory (Section 11).


                                                     7
                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


    This route gives the programme a way to formulate and audit particular scaling claims. It does not
establish the empirical propositions of Law I, nor does the occurrence of the same exponent in another
domain validate them. Section 16 gives the registered programme’s status. Appendix E places each
current contribution beside the closest earlier work used here; Appendix B preserves the dated record,
and Appendix A records the earlier wording and its corrections.

2.3   The strongest objection: assumptions chosen after the fit
The strongest objection is that the framework could recover a familiar exponent by choosing its operators,
dimension or transport assumptions after seeing the data. If a fitted slope is inverted to assign a dimension,
or an inventory is calculated from the identity that the study is meant to test, the apparent agreement adds
no empirical information. A correct conditional theorem can still be inapplicable to the system being
measured.
     The answer must be a measurement procedure, not a broader sufficiency claim. The system, variables
and eligible network class must be declared in advance. The demand, route-length and velocity assumptions
must be checked from measurements that do not merely reproduce the response being predicted, and the
prediction must be assessed on held-out networks with independently measured inventory. A precise
discrepancy under the declared premises counts against that model’s applicability; failure of a premise is
reported as a limit on its domain. The protocol must state these decisions before outcomes are examined.
Until such a test is completed, the new empirical contribution remains prospective.
     The narrower mathematical result is already available: once the flow model’s explicit premises hold,
its exponent follows. It replaces the earlier assertion that multiplicative composition, space filling and
conservation, without further specification, must force 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1). The comparison with prior models
is then a concrete question about their assumptions and observables, rather than a claim that different
physics has been proved to obey one universal mechanism.


3     Name the function and its variables
A scaling statement identifies two quantities and a range. The same system can have a power law in one
variable pair and an exponential in another. The following examples describe functions; they assign no
physical mechanism by themselves.
Definition 1 (Power law). For 𝑥 > 0 and 𝐴 > 0,

                                            𝑦 = 𝐴𝑥 𝑏 ,         𝑏 ∈ R.                                     (1)

Positive exponents below one are sublinear; one is linear; exponents above one are superlinear. Negative
powers decrease. These are all power laws.
Definition 2 (Exponential).
                                                  𝑦 = 𝐴𝑒 𝜆𝑥 .                                             (2)
Equal additive increments in 𝑥 multiply the output by the same factor. This is distinct from a superlinear
power law.
Definition 3 (One saturating response). For 𝑥 ≥ 0, 𝐾 > 0 and 𝑌max > 0,
                                                               𝑥
                                               𝑦 = 𝑌max           .                                       (3)
                                                             𝐾 +𝑥
This is one bounded increasing form, not the unique consequence of a physical limit. Other bounded
dynamics can converge differently or oscillate.

                                                         8
                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


    The logarithmic form belongs to the fourth operation pair in Section 4. Saturation requires a separate
dynamical or response model. Choosing among these forms requires a declared observable, normalisation,
error model and test; a visual resemblance is insufficient.


4     What the composition equations establish
A scaling explanation must specify what is combined and what the observable preserves. Classical
functional equations give an exact answer once those choices are fixed (Cauchy 1821; Aczél 1966). Write
𝐺 + = (R, +) for the additive real numbers and 𝐺 × = (R>0 , ×) for the multiplicative positive real numbers.
A homomorphism is a map that preserves the declared source and target operations.

Theorem 1 (Four continuous homomorphism problems). For each of the following source and target
pairs, every continuous homomorphism has the stated form for some 𝑐 ∈ R, and each stated function is a
homomorphism:
    1. 𝐹 : 𝐺 + → 𝐺 + preserves addition in both variables:

                            𝐹 (𝑥 + 𝑦) = 𝐹 (𝑥) + 𝐹 (𝑦),        𝐹 (𝑥) = 𝑐𝑥,       𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ R.                   (4)

    2. 𝐹 : 𝐺 × → 𝐺 × preserves multiplication in both variables:

                                𝐹 (𝑥𝑦) = 𝐹 (𝑥)𝐹 (𝑦),       𝐹 (𝑥) = 𝑥 𝑐 ,      𝑥, 𝑦 > 0.                     (5)

    3. 𝐹 : 𝐺 + → 𝐺 × takes input addition to output multiplication:

                             𝐹 (𝑥 + 𝑦) = 𝐹 (𝑥)𝐹 (𝑦),        𝐹 (𝑥) = 𝑒 𝑐𝑥 ,     𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ R.                    (6)

    4. 𝐹 : 𝐺 × → 𝐺 + takes input multiplication to output addition:

                            𝐹 (𝑥𝑦) = 𝐹 (𝑥) + 𝐹 (𝑦),        𝐹 (𝑥) = 𝑐 log 𝑥,      𝑥, 𝑦 > 0.                  (7)

The logarithm is natural. The additive target is real-valued, not restricted to positive outputs. The constant
cases are included: 𝑐 = 0 gives the constant zero map into 𝐺 + and the constant one map into 𝐺 × . A
zero-valued function is not a map into 𝐺 × .

4.1    The proof and the scope of the four cells
Proof. First suppose 𝐻 : R → R is continuous and additive. The identity at zero gives 𝐻 (0) = 0;
applying it to 𝑥 + (−𝑥) gives 𝐻 (−𝑥) = −𝐻 (𝑥). Repeated addition gives 𝐻 (𝑛𝑥) = 𝑛𝐻 (𝑥) for every integer
𝑛. For a positive integer 𝑛, 𝑛𝐻 (1/𝑛) = 𝐻 (1), so 𝐻 (𝑚/𝑛) = (𝑚/𝑛)𝐻 (1) for every integer 𝑚. Thus
𝐻 (𝑞) = 𝑐𝑞 on the rationals, where 𝑐 = 𝐻 (1). For any real 𝑥, choose rationals 𝑞 𝑗 → 𝑥. Continuity gives
𝐻 (𝑥) = lim 𝑗 𝐻 (𝑞 𝑗 ) = 𝑐𝑥.
    This proves the first row. For the multiplicative row define 𝐻 (𝑡) = log 𝐹 (𝑒 𝑡 ) for all real 𝑡. Positivity
makes the logarithm defined, and the identity for 𝐹 gives 𝐻 (𝑠 + 𝑡) = 𝐻 (𝑠) + 𝐻 (𝑡). Continuity therefore
gives 𝐻 (𝑡) = 𝑐𝑡, and substituting 𝑡 = log 𝑥 gives 𝐹 (𝑥) = 𝑥 𝑐 .
    For the third row, 𝐻 (𝑡) = log 𝐹 (𝑡) is continuous and additive on R, hence 𝐹 (𝑡) = 𝑒 𝑐𝑡 . For the
fourth, 𝐻 (𝑡) = 𝐹 (𝑒 𝑡 ) is continuous and additive, hence 𝐹 (𝑥) = 𝑐 log 𝑥. Direct substitution verifies every
converse, including the constant cases.                                                                       □




                                                       9
                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


     Four counts the chosen source and target operation pairs. It does not count all possible physical laws
or four disjoint sets of numerical functions. The power-law exponent may be negative, zero, sub-linear
or super-linear. A critical power law can have the power-law form; this theorem neither excludes it nor
derives its critical exponent or physical mechanism. Other operations and other domains pose different
classification problems.
     Normalisation is part of the claim. If 𝑌 (𝑥) = 𝐴𝑥 𝑐 with 𝐴 > 0, then 𝑌 (𝑥𝑦) = 𝑌 (𝑥)𝑌 (𝑦)/𝐴. Its
normalised response 𝐹 (𝑥) = 𝑌 (𝑥)/𝑌 (1) satisfies Equation (5). For an exponential measured from a
first-pass baseline, 𝐺 (𝑅) = 𝑒 𝑐 (𝑅−1) has 𝐺 (1) = 1; its additive coordinate is 𝑟 = 𝑅 − 1. For a baseline-one
logarithmic response 𝐺 (𝑅) = 1 + 𝑐 log 𝑅, the additive output is 𝐺 − 1, not 𝐺 itself. Changing a coordinate
or baseline can change the equation being tested.

4.2   A precise consequence for approximate composition
The following statement applies additive Hyers stability in declared logarithmic coordinates (Hyers 1941).
It specifies both the error norm and its domain.

Proposition 1 (Global logarithmic-defect bound). Let 𝐹 : R>0 → R>0 be continuous. If a constant 𝜀 ≥ 0
satisfies
                      |log 𝐹 (𝑥𝑦) − log 𝐹 (𝑥) − log 𝐹 (𝑦)| ≤ 𝜀 for every 𝑥, 𝑦 > 0,                (8)
then there is a unique 𝑐 ∈ R for which

                      |log 𝐹 (𝑥) − 𝑐 log 𝑥| ≤ 𝜀,      𝑒 − 𝜀 𝑥 𝑐 ≤ 𝐹 (𝑥) ≤ 𝑒 𝜀 𝑥 𝑐   (𝑥 > 0).                 (9)

Proof. Put ℎ(𝑡) = log 𝐹 (𝑒 𝑡 ) and ℎ 𝑛 (𝑡) = 2−𝑛 ℎ(2𝑛 𝑡). The hypothesis gives

                                         |ℎ 𝑛+1 (𝑡) − ℎ 𝑛 (𝑡)| ≤ 2−𝑛−1 𝜀

for every real 𝑡. Summing this bound shows that ℎ 𝑛 converges uniformly to a continuous function 𝐴, with
| 𝐴(𝑡) − ℎ(𝑡)| ≤ 𝜀. Also
                                  |ℎ 𝑛 (𝑠 + 𝑡) − ℎ 𝑛 (𝑠) − ℎ 𝑛 (𝑡)| ≤ 2−𝑛 𝜀.
Passing to the limit proves that 𝐴 is additive. The rational and continuity argument above gives 𝐴(𝑡) = 𝑐𝑡.
If two constants 𝑐 1 , 𝑐 2 met the uniform bound, then |(𝑐 1 − 𝑐 2 )𝑡| ≤ 2𝜀 for every real 𝑡, forcing 𝑐 1 = 𝑐 2 .
Substitution of 𝑡 = log 𝑥 and exponentiation give the result.                                                □

    An absolute residual |𝐹 (𝑥𝑦) − 𝐹 (𝑥)𝐹 (𝑦)| is a different error metric. Observations of finitely many
noisy pairs cannot establish the global bound in Equation (8). The proposition contains no dynamics that
attract a system towards a family. Its conclusion is proximity under a stated hypothesis, not a claim that
perturbations in nature must take this form.

4.3   Scaling limits and regular variation
Suppose 𝐹 is positive and measurable on the positive reals and, for every 𝑥 > 0, the finite positive limit

                                                      𝑡→∞   𝐹 (𝑡)
exists. Then
                                           𝐹 (𝑡𝑥𝑦) 𝐹 (𝑡𝑥𝑦) 𝐹 (𝑡𝑥)
                                                  =
                                            𝐹 (𝑡)   𝐹 (𝑡𝑥) 𝐹 (𝑡)

                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


                          Table 1. Assumptions that must accompany a composition claim.

Choice                               What must be stated

gives 𝐻 (𝑥𝑦) = 𝐻 (𝑥)𝐻 (𝑦), because 𝑡𝑥 → ∞ for fixed 𝑥 > 0. The limit is measurable, for example by
taking the sequence 𝑡 = 𝑛. The classical measurable Cauchy result then gives 𝐻 (𝑥) = 𝑥 𝑐 . Writing
L (𝑡) = 𝐹 (𝑡)/𝑡 𝑐 yields

                                                  L (𝑡𝑥)
                           𝐹 (𝑡) = 𝑡 𝑐 L (𝑡),            −→ 1      for every fixed 𝑥 > 0.

This is the regular-variation characterisation, with L a slowly varying factor (Bingham et al. 1987). An
asymptotic power ratio need not be an exact power at finite scale. For example, at large 𝑡, 𝑡 𝑐 log 𝑡 has the
same limiting power ratios but local logarithmic slope 𝑐 + 1/log 𝑡.
    The existence and positivity of the limits are hypotheses, not conclusions about every measured
curve. Generalised functional equations and homomorphisms form part of the established setting for
these questions (Ostaszewski 2016). The empirical contribution sought here is to justify the equation or
limit before inspecting the validation curve, then test the resulting prediction against alternatives.


5    The principle and its empirical test
A recursive update describes a succession of states, 𝑄 𝑛+1 = 𝑇 (𝑄 𝑛 , 𝑢 𝑛 ), where 𝑢 𝑛 names any additional
input. A composition equation instead relates measurements made at combined inputs. To obtain a scaling
prediction, a model must connect the update procedure to the observable equation. Dependence on an
earlier state, or associativity of a chosen operation, alone does not provide that connection.
    The two operations must both be named. Multiplication of input scale ratios together with multiplication
of normalised outputs gives the power-law row of Theorem 1. Addition of input intervals together with
multiplication of positive outputs gives its exponential row. Addition of both inputs and outputs gives a
linear map; multiplication of inputs together with addition of real outputs gives a logarithm. These are
statements about observables, not labels that a substrate acquires merely by branching or feeding back.
    For example, the state rule 𝑄 𝑛+1 = 𝑞𝑄 𝑛 with 𝑞 > 0 and 𝑄 0 > 0 at integer ticks gives 𝑄 𝑛 = 𝑄 0 𝑞 𝑛 .
Although the state is multiplied at every update, the input tick count adds, and the normalised output is
exponential in that count. Calling the update “multiplicative” without identifying the input operation
would give the wrong classification.

A bound and convergence are different premises. A bounded sequence can oscillate: 𝑄 𝑛 = 2 + (−1) 𝑛
never converges. For the particular capped update

                            𝑄 𝑛+1 = min(𝑄 𝑛 + 𝛿 𝑛 , 𝐾),         0 ≤ 𝑄 0 ≤ 𝐾,    𝛿 𝑛 ≥ 0,



                                                           11
                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


monotonicity and boundedness together imply convergence to a limit at most 𝐾. In fact 𝑄 𝑛 =
min(𝑄 0 + 𝑗<𝑛 𝛿 𝑗 , 𝐾). Its limit equals 𝐾 only when the cumulative increments reach or exceed the
          ∑︁
remaining gap; finite-time attainment requires a finite partial sum to do so. Different bounded dynamics
can approach a limit at different rates, or fail to approach any limit. Saturation therefore requires its own
dynamics and is not a fifth Cauchy cell.

The ARC Principle. The ARC Principle remains Law I of the ARC Theory, a law under test, with its
statement and verdict rules fixed by the theory registration. This paper asks whether a justified composition
description can predict later scaling. The classical theorem supplies conditional forms; a scientific test
must establish why the relevant observable obeys the proposed equation and whether that description
predicts held-out observations better than rivals. Neither the theorem nor a retrospective fit supplies a
registered verdict. The measurement requirements are set out in Section 15.


6     Where the network exponent comes from
A network’s dimension describes its geometry. To predict how much it transports, we also need the
demand it serves and the distance over which it carries that demand. The following model separates these
quantities. It gives a conditional derivation of the familiar fraction 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) and identifies the additional
measurement needed before that fraction can describe metabolism against body mass.

6.1   An exact accounting identity
Consider a finite rooted tree that carries a resource from one source to sinks 𝑖. Each sink receives a
steady non-negative rate 𝑞 𝑖 , with at least one 𝑞 𝑖 > 0. Edge 𝑒 has length ℓ𝑒 ≥ 0 and carries the sum of the
demands downstream of it:                               ∑︁
                                                𝑄𝑒 =         𝑞𝑖 ,                                        (11)

where 𝑃𝑖 is the route from the source to sink 𝑖. Flow is conserved at intermediate junctions; the resource
counted here is neither created nor consumed before reaching its designated sink. Define total throughput
𝐵, flow-length cost 𝐶 and the demand-weighted mean route length ℓ¯𝑞 by

time. It is a flow-length cost, not automatically energy expenditure, network mass or body mass.

Lemma 1 (Flow accounting). Under these definitions,

                                                     𝐶 = 𝐵ℓ¯𝑞 .                                          (13)

    The same accounting applies to a network with split routes when every non-negative route flow and

                                     𝐶 = 𝐵ℓ¯𝑞 + 𝐶circ ,     𝐶circ ≥ 0.                               (15)
Here ℓ¯𝑞 refers only to the specified source-to-sink routes. Alternatively, recirculation that belongs to a
delivered resource’s itinerary can be included in that itinerary’s full length. Counting only its shortest
path while charging the actual circulating edge flows would mix two different models.

6.2   Demand and route length select the exponent
Let 𝐿 denote a dimensionless size coordinate, such as the ratio of a domain’s linear extent to a fixed
reference length. All dimensional scales below are carried by positive constants 𝐵0 and ℓ0 .
Proposition 2 (Conditional throughput scaling). For a family satisfying Eq. (13), suppose that, as 𝐿 → ∞,

                         𝐵(𝐿)               ℓ¯𝑞 (𝐿)

An asymptotic exponent is not a finite-range prediction. For dimensionless 𝐿 > 0 and a constant
𝐴 > 0, take 𝐵/𝐵0 = 𝐿 + 𝐴 and ℓ¯𝑞 /ℓ0 = 𝐿. Then 𝐶/𝐶0 = 𝐿 (𝐿 + 𝐴), and both input scalings have limiting
exponent one. The asymptotic throughput exponent against 𝐶 is 1/2, but direct differentiation gives the

                                       d log 𝐵     𝐿       1

A finite-range fit need not recover the limit. A predictive experiment must therefore lock either the
complete finite-range functions and their uncertainty or explicit bounds on the finite-size corrections.
Simply inserting two limiting exponents into Equation (17) does not supply that prediction.
    The geometric special case is therefore
                                         𝑑
                                 𝛼𝐶 =               when 𝜇 = 𝑑 and 𝜁 = 1.                                (19)

Both premises need a reason. Suppose a 𝑑-dimensional family has a number of sinks proportional to 𝐿 𝑑
and a mean demand per sink tending to a positive size-independent value. Then total demand has exponent
𝜇 = 𝑑. Constant sink density in an expanding domain is one way to obtain the required sink count. A
change in density or demand per sink changes this conclusion even when the embedding dimension stays
fixed.
     Route scaling needs an equally explicit condition. Suppose a fixed positive fraction of total demand
lies at distance at least 𝑐𝐿ℓ∗ from the source, where 𝑐 > 0 and ℓ∗ is a fixed length, while every served route
has length at most 𝐾 𝐿ℓ∗ for a size-independent 𝐾. The lower distance condition and the upper route bound
place ℓ¯𝑞 between positive constant multiples of 𝐿. Within the power-scaling assumptions of Eq. (16), they
force 𝜁 = 1. Bounds alone do not supply a limiting prefactor; the stronger asymptotic relation also requires

                                                      13
                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


convergence of the normalised mean route length, for example through a specified self-similar family.
Demand concentrated near the source, increasing path stretch or unaccounted recirculation changes the
premises.
    The demand-distance argument also gives a conditional bound. If the positive fraction at distance at
least 𝑐𝐿ℓ∗ is retained but the upper route bound is dropped, then ℓ¯𝑞 is at least a positive constant times 𝐿.
Under Eq. (16), 𝜁 ≥ 1. With 𝜇 = 𝑑 > 0, this implies 𝛼𝐶 ≤ 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1). The bound concerns the declared
throughput and flow-length cost; its hypotheses are part of the result.

6.3   Inventory and body mass are separate quantities
An inventory relation can be derived when the transported resource moves at a common speed 𝑣 on every
edge. In steady flow, the amount in transit on edge 𝑒 is its rate 𝑄 𝑒 multiplied by its transit time ℓ𝑒 /𝑣. Thus
                                                   ∑︁        ℓ𝑒 𝐶

                                                    𝑒

to the residence-time accounting. If edge speeds differ, the exact expression is instead 𝑁 = 𝑒 𝑄 𝑒 ℓ𝑒 /𝑣 𝑒 ;

an arbitrary average speed cannot replace the 𝑣 𝑒 .

The constants in Eq. (22) carry the units. Neither 𝜂 = 1 nor a proportionality between body mass and
inventory follows from geometric dimension. A measured metabolic quantity must also correspond to the
resource throughput being modelled.
    The velocity dependence is explicit in Banavar et al. (2010). Their radial and hierarchically branched
models supply a three-dimensional volume. They obtain a metabolic mass exponent of 2/3 when flow
velocity is independent of body size, and an upper value of 3/4 when velocity scales as 𝑀 1/12 under their
additional service-volume constraints. Their assumptions make the inventory in transit proportional to
mass and route length proportional to 𝑀 1/3 . In that setting the accounting reads
                                           𝑀𝑣
                                     𝐵∝        ∝ 𝑀 2/3+𝜈 ,           𝑣 ∝ 𝑀𝜈.                               (24)
                                           ℓ¯𝑞

This explains the two exponents without changing the spatial dimension. The permissible upper value of 𝜈
is supplied by that model’s constraints, not by the accounting identity. Its demand and mass assumptions
must not be silently replaced by 𝜇 = 𝑑 and 𝑀 ∝ 𝐶.


                                                        14
                             The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


6.4   What a cross-domain comparison tests
The useful prediction is now explicit: independently characterised demand and routing determine a
throughput-cost exponent, and a separately justified bridge determines whether it is also a mass exponent.
A comparison must state the two measured variables, the size range, physiological or physical conditions,
and the uncertainty in each premise. Computing 𝐶 from 𝐵ℓ¯𝑞 and then recovering the same identity is an
accounting check; evidence for the model comes from independent measurements of the premises and a
prediction of outcomes not used to choose them.
    Biological measurements distinguish these possibilities. White and Seymour (2003) report mammalian
basal-metabolism fits compatible with 2/3 after temperature adjustment and restrictions on eligible
measurements. Kolokotrones et al. (2010) report curvature in mammalian metabolic scaling, so a fitted
constant exponent depends on the mass range. These results concern defined datasets and measurement
conditions. Source-specific values and methods belong with those definitions in Section 12; selected
means do not supply a new validation of Eq. (19).
    The same requirement applies outside biology. A roughness exponent, a distribution-tail exponent,
a throughput exponent and a cosmological scale-factor exponent describe different variable pairs. An
analytic equality between selected fractions supplies no measurement of demand or routing. Section 9
gives a complete example: its powers follow from the fluid equations, while the apparent network
dimension is introduced afterwards by a change of variables. The transport result remains a definite
conditional statement, with a mechanism and observables that can be specified before a test.


7     What metabolic scaling implies for the heartbeat
The heart supplies oxygen by moving blood, but a beat is not a fixed quantity of oxygen delivery across
animals. Stroke volume and the oxygen extracted from each unit of blood connect metabolic demand to
heart rate. Writing that balance explicitly shows what is required for a quarter-power relation.

7.1   The oxygen balance
Let 𝑉¤O2 denote whole-body oxygen consumption per unit time, 𝑓 𝐻 heart rate, 𝑉𝑠 stroke volume and Δ𝑂 2
the difference between arterial and mixed-venous oxygen content per unit blood volume. At steady state,
with no net accumulation of the oxygen being accounted for, consumption equals blood flow multiplied by
the oxygen removed from that flow. Since cardiac output is 𝑓 𝐻 𝑉𝑠 , the Fick balance is

                                               𝑉¤O2 = 𝑓 𝐻 𝑉𝑠 Δ𝑂 2 .                                  (25)

The units are oxygen amount per unit time on each side: beats per unit time, multiplied by blood volume
per beat and oxygen amount per blood volume. This is a conservation relation, not a spatial scaling law.
    Use the dimensionless mass 𝑚 = 𝑀/𝑀0 and suppose, over a declared range and comparable
physiological conditions, that

                          𝑉¤O2 = 𝑉¤0 𝑚 𝑏 ,     𝑉𝑠 = 𝑉𝑠0 𝑚 𝑠 ,         Δ𝑂 2 = Δ𝑂 20 𝑚 𝑒 ,             (26)

with positive prefactors. Substitution gives

                                                                                   𝑉¤0
                         𝑓 𝐻 = 𝑓 𝐻0 𝑚 ℎ ,      ℎ = 𝑏 − 𝑠 − 𝑒,          𝑓 𝐻0 =             .          (27)
                                                                                𝑉𝑠0 Δ𝑂 20
An asymptotic version follows if the three input relations are asymptotic rather than exact powers. In
either case, the familiar ℎ = −1/4 follows from the three scaling assumptions 𝑏 = 3/4, 𝑠 = 1 and 𝑒 = 0.

                                                       15
                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


The oxygen consumed grows as the three-quarter power, stroke volume grows in proportion to mass, and
oxygen extraction per unit blood volume is independent of mass. A metabolic-energy exponent can stand
in for 𝑏 only when its conversion to oxygen consumption is justified for the measurements in question.
    Mass-specific oxygen consumption is a different quantity:

                                              𝑉¤O2   𝑉¤0 𝑏−1
                                                   =    𝑚 .                                            (28)
                                               𝑀     𝑀0
Its exponent equals the heart-rate exponent only if 𝑠 + 𝑒 = 1. The balance therefore gives a testable
relation between three physiological scalings; dimension alone supplies neither an individual’s pulse nor
its prefactor.

7.2   Physiological state is part of the observation
Goldbogen et al. (2019) recorded one male blue whale for 8.5 hours. Diving heart rates were typically 4
to 8 beats per minute, with a minimum of 2; post-dive surface rates reached 25 to 37. Their approximately
15 beats per minute resting value was an allometric prediction, not a direct measurement of a resting
constant. These observations show why diving, recovery and resting values must retain their conditions.
They are not interchangeable points in a cross-species resting-heart-rate fit.
    A comparison between species must therefore state the activity and thermal conditions, mass
measurements, reference ranges and uncertainty, and must establish the stroke-volume and extraction
assumptions relevant to that comparison. Choosing one representative pulse for each animal and supplying
one animal’s value as a prefactor does not independently test a prediction from network dimension.

7.3   Lifetime totals require a lifetime average
The number of beats over a lifespan 𝜏 is the integral of heart rate over that life:

                                                                   1
                                ∫ 𝜏                                  ∫ 𝜏
                      𝑁beats =       𝑓 𝐻 (𝑡) d𝑡 = 𝑓¯𝐻 𝜏,    𝑓¯𝐻 =          𝑓 𝐻 (𝑡) d𝑡.                 (29)
                                 0                                 𝜏 0

Here 𝑓¯𝐻 is the lifetime mean, not a resting measurement. If that mean and a consistently defined lifespan
scale as 𝑚 ℎ̄ and 𝑚 ℓ , their product scales as 𝑚 ℎ̄+ℓ . Cancellation requires ℎ̄ + ℓ = 0. It removes a mass
exponent within the specified model; it does not establish a universal numerical total across species,
activity histories or definitions of lifespan. The prefactors and the replacement of a lifetime mean by a
convenient resting value remain separate empirical questions.


8     The fission chain reaction
The rest-energy relation 𝐸 = 𝑚𝑐2 (Einstein 1905) and the kinetics of a chain reaction answer different
questions. A reaction’s energy release depends on the difference between initial and final states. In a
simplified branching description, the expected number of events in generation 𝑛 obeys 𝑁 𝑛+1 = 𝑘 𝑁 𝑛 , with
a fixed effective multiplication factor 𝑘. Then 𝑁 𝑛 = 𝑁0 𝑘 𝑛 is exponential in additive generation count.
This is a mathematical description under a fixed-factor premise, not a claim that an actual transient keeps
that factor constant.

What the grid does and does not explain: the chain reaction. A branching model grows exponentially
while its effective multiplication factor exceeds one and stays constant. That is a branching process, and
the arithmetic was understood in the late 1930s; it owes nothing to this work. In the four-cell grid it


                                                      16
                              The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


                                    Table 2. The chain reaction’s regimes.

Regime          𝑘      Cell of the grid                             Behaviour            Status
Subcritical     𝑘<1    Additive input, multiplicative output        Exponential decay    Classified, not
                                                                                         predicted
Supercritical   𝑘>1    Additive input, multiplicative output        Exponential          Classified, not
                                                                    growth               predicted
Controlled      𝑘≈1    Bounded by feedback (outside the grid)       Steady power         Classified, not
                                                                                         predicted


sits in the additive-input, multiplicative-output cell, so the grid classifies it correctly. Classifying is not
explaining. The physical mechanism and the conditions that sustain its effective factor must be supplied
separately; naming the cell adds no physical content.

9     Cosmic expansion and the meaning of a shared formula
Cosmology provides a precise example of the difference between deriving a power and relabelling it.
The expansion law follows from gravitational dynamics and the fluid’s equation of state. Expressing its
exponent as 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) afterwards does not supply an independently measured transport dimension.

9.1   The fluid equations determine the expansion
Consider an expanding, spatially flat, homogeneous and isotropic model with one positive-density perfect
fluid and constant equation-of-state parameter 𝑤. Write 𝑃 = 𝑤𝜌𝑐2 , with 𝜌 the mass-equivalent density, 𝑃
pressure and 𝑐 the speed of light. There is no additional component in this single-fluid solution. With
𝐻 = ¤𝑎/𝑎, the Friedmann and continuity equations give (Carroll et al. 2003)
                                                  8𝜋𝐺
                                          𝐻2 =         𝜌,                                                  (30)

                                             𝜌¤ = −3𝐻 (1 + 𝑤) 𝜌,                                           (31)
                                            𝑎¥     4𝜋𝐺

                                            𝑎        3
For 𝑤 > −1, integrating on the expanding branch gives
                                              )︃ 𝑝

                         𝑎(𝑡) = 𝑎 ∗                , 𝑝=          ,           𝑡 > 𝑡0 ,                      (33)

where 𝑎 ∗ > 0 and 𝑡∗ > 0 are reference scales consistent with the density normalisation. One way to see

equation makes 𝑎 3(1+𝑤)/2 proportional to 𝑡 − 𝑡 0 .
    The case 𝑤 = −1 is solved separately. Its density is constant, so the expanding branch has constant

9.2    Acceleration and exponential expansion are distinct
The deceleration parameter of the single-fluid model is
                                                          𝑎 𝑎¥ 1 + 3𝑤
                                              𝑞 dec = −        =      .                                          (35)
                                                          𝑎¤ 2    2
Positive density therefore gives acceleration when 𝑤 < −1/3. At 𝑤 = −1/3, the power exponent is 𝑝 = 1
and the solution coasts linearly. For −1 < 𝑤 < −1/3, it is an accelerating power law. In particular,
𝑤 = −2/3 gives 𝑝 = 2 and 𝑎(𝑡) ∝ (𝑡 − 𝑡 0 ) 2 . Exponential expansion occurs at 𝑤 = −1 under the
assumptions above (Carroll et al. 2003).
     Version 3.13 already corrected the earlier identification of the acceleration boundary with a change
from power-law to exponential expansion. That correction is retained here. The quadratic example makes
its consequence explicit: acceleration does not require an exponential. A linear function is itself a member
of the power-law family, not a boundary of that family’s mathematical existence.

9.3    An algebraic coordinate, not a measured dimension
For a finite power exponent 𝑝 ≠ 1, define
                                              𝑝      2                      𝑑eff
                                   𝑑eff =        =       ,          𝑝=             .                             (36)
                                            1 − 𝑝 1 + 3𝑤                  𝑑eff + 1
In this section 𝑑eff is solely the coordinate defined by that equation. It is not the independently specified
geometric 𝑑 in Section 6. Within the expanding power-law branch, the map is finite for 𝑤 > −1 except at
𝑤 = −1/3. It is positive for 𝑤 > −1/3 and less than −1 for −1 < 𝑤 < −1/3.
    At 𝑤 = −1/3, the map has a pole while the physical solution remains linear and regular. Approaching
the boundary from the decelerating side sends 𝑑eff to positive infinity; approaching from the accelerating
side sends it to negative infinity. Formally substituting 𝑤 = −1 gives 𝑑eff = −1, where the expression
for 𝑝 is undefined. The exponential solution therefore has no finite power exponent represented by this
coordinate.
Table 3. Analytic cases of the flat, constant, single-fluid model. The entries follow from its equations; they are not
measured network dimensions or independent confirmations of a network law.

                   Fluid or case               𝑤     Time exponent 𝑝       𝑞 dec            𝑑eff
                   Stiff fluid                 1             1/3             2              1/2
                   Radiation                  1/3            1/2             1               1
                   Pressureless matter         0             2/3           1/2               2
                   Coasting                  −1/3             1              0          Undefined
                   Accelerating example      −2/3             2            −1/2             −2
                   Vacuum                     −1          Exponential       −1         Not applicable


    The radiation and pressureless-matter powers follow from 𝑤 = 1/3 and 𝑤 = 0 in the fluid equations.
Those equations assume a homogeneous background; neither a photon-path network nor a cosmic-web
partition enters their derivation. Interpreting the numbers 1 and 2 in the last column as such mechanisms
would add a premise that has not been established by the change of variables. Nor does the cosmic web
supply those numbers. The flat, matter-only model of Einstein and de Sitter (1932) is the case 𝑤 = 0 of
Table 3, and the matter era it idealises began at matter-radiation equality, at redshift 𝑧 eq = 3402 ± 26 in
the baseline results of Planck Collaboration (2020, Table 2). The web is “the largest scale manifestation
of the anisotropic gravitational collapse of matter” and “consists of the largest non-linear structures in

                                                           18
                             The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


the Universe” (Cautun et al. 2014); it formed by that collapse, long after the matter era began. In it, the
analysis of Cautun et al. (2014) “identifies clusters and filaments as the most prominent components of
the web”, while “voids and sheets take most of the volume”.

9.4   Idealised components and cosmic history
A single constant 𝑤 is an idealisation, not a complete history of a universe containing several evolving
contributions. The powers in Table 3 describe the stated one-component models and can describe limiting
regimes when their assumptions apply. A mixture, an evolving equation of state, spatial curvature or the
branch with 𝑤 < −1 requires its own solution. A change in the dominant component does not, by itself,
establish a change in an independently measured composition operator.

9.5   What the common form establishes
Equation (36) is a reparametrisation of an exponent already derived from cosmology. It supplies no extra
equation for the expansion, and fitting 𝑑eff from the same power cannot be an independent prediction of
that power. In the transport calculation, by contrast, the proposed exponent depends on demand, routes
and an observable bridge that must be specified before their prediction is tested.
    The connection that survives is exact and useful: a shared functional form can organise a comparison
while leaving the physical derivations distinct. A claim of a common mechanism would require additional
equations and independently identified quantities linking the two domains. The fluid calculation itself
supplies no biological prediction, and a biological exponent is not a measurement of the cosmological
coordinate in Eq. (36).

Proposition 3 (Demand at macroscopic distance). Suppose fixed constants 0 < 𝑝 ≤ 1 and 𝑐 > 0,
independent of 𝐿, have the following property for all sufficiently large networks: sinks at Euclidean

                                                           𝜇

Proof. Every route to those sinks has length at least their source-to-sink distance. Hence ℓ¯𝑞 ≥ 𝑝𝑐𝐿. If
𝜁 < 1, the assumed relation ℓ¯𝑞 ∼ ℓ0 𝐿 𝜁 would give ℓ¯𝑞 /𝐿 → 0, contradicting this lower bound. Thus 𝜁 ≥ 1,
and the inequality follows from 𝜇 > 0.                                                                   □

    Setting 𝜇 = 𝑑 needs an additional demand assumption. For example, if the number of sinks is
asymptotic to 𝑛0 𝐿 𝑑 under fixed sink density and their average demand tends to a positive constant 𝑞 0 ,
then 𝐵 ∼ 𝑛0 𝑞 0 𝐿 𝑑 . Embedding a network in a 𝑑-dimensional domain alone does not fix its sink number or
demand. If, in addition to the lower bound above, route stretch is bounded so that ℓ¯𝑞 ≤ 𝐾 𝐿 with fixed 𝐾,
the stated power asymptotic has 𝜁 = 1. Only with both choices does 𝛼𝐶 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) follow as an equality.

    To transfer this result to another response variable requires another model. If body mass satisfies
𝑀 ∼ 𝑚 0𝐶 𝜂 with 𝑚 0 , 𝜂 > 0, the corresponding exponent is 𝑏 𝑀 = 𝛼𝐶 /𝜂, not automatically 𝛼𝐶 . At a
specified common transport velocity 𝑣, inventory in transit is 𝐶/𝑣; scale-dependent velocity changes
that bridge. The physical assumptions in transport explanations must therefore be compared directly
(West et al. 1997; Banavar et al. 1999, 2010). An observed exponent above one for a different variable
pair contradicts neither Proposition 3 nor finite-dimensional geometry. Systems that do not meet these
premises can also exceed one: prokaryote metabolic rate scales superlinearly with mass (DeLong et al.
2010), and quantities reflecting wealth creation and innovation in cities scale superlinearly with population
(Bettencourt et al. 2007). The limit 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) → 1 as 𝑑 → ∞ is a limit of this formula, not a physical
phase boundary.


11     Recursive capability and correction
The transport exponent of Section 10 concerns demand against a flow-weighted transport quantity.
Capability against recursive depth is another observable pair. A capability exponent above one would not
be an escape from a physical law; it would require its own model and measurement, with the hardware,
energy, latency and evaluation costs still accounted for.

11.1    A fixed rule can generate super-linear growth
The unchanged rule
                                               √
                                      𝑥 𝑛+1 = ( 𝑥 𝑛 + 1) 2 ,     𝑥0 = 1,                                  (40)
gives 𝑥 𝑛 = (𝑛 + 1) 2 for every integer 𝑛 ≥ 0. The initial value has that form, and substituting (𝑛 + 1) 2
into the rule gives (𝑛 + 2) 2 . This is a counterexample to the mathematical inference that fixed rules force
sub-linearity; it is not an AI measurement.

                                                      20
                             The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


    Frozen model weights, fixed agent code, persistent memory, prompts, tools and the procedure that
proposes subsequent improvements are separate experimental choices. A change to any one does not
guarantee improvement or faster improvement in the next round. A test must identify what changes, what
remains fixed, the available resources and the outcome. The error-decay fit reported in Paper II remains
inconclusive, and its earlier super-linear estimate remains retracted; neither result is replaced by this
counterexample.

divergence.

11.3    Elapsed time, correction and the conditional ceiling
Depth and time cannot be interchanged without a clock model. Paper XIII defines self-acceleration
through cycle duration proportional to capability to the power −𝛿, with constant 𝛿 ≥ 0 on a fixed substrate.
With fixed parameters and that duration law, combining the gain ODE with elapsed time gives 𝑘 = 𝛿 − 1/𝛼
in a capability-growth law proportional to 𝑈 1+𝑘 . Hardware procurement, a proportional overhead and
an irreducible additive serial latency are not the same clock change. An additive latency changes the
local self-acceleration exponent; the constant-exponent clock law must therefore be checked rather than
inferred from faster completion alone.
     Paper X separately models departure magnitude 𝐷, the normalised index 𝐷/𝑈, correction strength
and capability growth. Its correction exponent, denoted 𝛽𝐶 here, is not the gain coupling 𝛽 𝐿 . With
positive gain drift, no level drift and the specified power-law correction model, 𝛽𝐶 > 𝑘 is the condition for
the modelled fraction to vanish. With positive gain and level drift the stated condition is 𝛽𝐶 > max(𝑘, 0).
These results assume the model’s forcing and correction laws; an unbounded power-law corrector is
a substantive premise. At equality, and with compounding, coefficients and forcing matter. Delay,
finite capacity and a change of measurement clock require their own analysis. A vanishing fraction can
accompany growing absolute 𝐷, and neither quantity is itself a measure of harm. A regular equation in a
depth coordinate does not guarantee enough wall-clock time for human intervention.
     The registered conditional pacing relation of Law III, the ARC Ceiling, a law under test, is
                                                 1
                                      𝛼crit =       ,        0 ≤ 𝛾 < 1.                                 (46)
                                                1−𝛾
It requires the specified burden model and zero direct-depth term; it is not derived here from a joint
model of capability, correction accumulation, covariance, delay and absolute harm. On the displayed
domain, 𝛼crit ≤ 2 is equivalent to 𝛾 ≤ 1/2. The same-class inequality carried by P6 with P20, and the
conjectured equality at one half, remain distinct from the ODE relation in Equation (42). Their similar
algebra supplies no independent confirmation. The theory registration and its frozen verdict rules remain
authoritative; this section adds no verdict or rule and establishes no universal ceiling of two.

11.4    Quantum error correction: distinguish the axes
The surface-code result reports logical error per cycle against code distance, with suppression factor
Λ = 2.14 ± 0.02 per distance increase of two (Google Quantum AI and Collaborators 2025). The preprint
was public from 24 August 2024 and the result announced on 9 December 2024; its relation to this
programme is convergent timing, not a prediction. Code distance, physical-qubit count and elapsed
correction cycles are different inputs. The author correction published on 28 April 2026 changes the label
of Figure 3a from surface-code distance to repetition-code distance and corrects its legend keys; it is not a
change of that horizontal axis into elapsed cycles (Google Quantum AI and Collaborators 2025).
    A stable per-cycle error rate therefore does not imply saturation of cumulative failure at a harmless
ceiling. These are established or conditional error-accounting relationships, not proof of the proposed
capability model. Likewise, a favourable capability or correction exponent demonstrates neither intrinsic
ethical behaviour nor a deployed safety guarantee. Evaluation must specify the values and tasks, measure
usefulness alongside correction, and retain human oversight, containment and shutdown for as long as
they can be held. Embedded correction is the programme’s contingency for control lost against our will,
whose effectiveness still requires the relevant tests.

12     Evidence, with its measurement and scope
The mathematical statements in Sections 4 and 6 follow from their premises. Applying them requires
separate evidence for those premises. An analytic identity, a fitted slope and a prospectively tested
prediction are different kinds of support.

12.1    Biological comparisons
The source-specific metabolic estimates and their measurement conditions are retained in Appendix F.
They are not pooled into a new estimate of a universal exponent. In particular, the fungal table mixes
biomass with radial extension; its former mean and tests do not establish one transport scaling relation. A
non-significant test against one half is not an equivalence result. The earlier three-group ANOVA and
model comparisons also relied on a group subsequently excluded from the proposed network class. Those
comparisons are retained as superseded history and carry no current validation weight.
    Note on the 34 debate. The empirical value of the mammalian metabolic scaling exponent is debated,
with estimates ranging from approximately 0.67 to 0.75 depending on taxon, mass range, temperature
correction, and statistical method (White and Seymour 2003; Glazier 2005, 2022). White and Seymour’s
data set of 619 species gives an exponent of 0.69 and, once body temperature and digestive state are
allowed for, exponents not significantly different from 23 (White and Seymour 2003); Savage et al. (2004)
find 0.737 for 626 species with the data binned by mass (0.712 unbinned); and Kolokotrones et al. (2010)
find the relation convex on logarithmic axes, and so not a pure power law, and report that a model based
on vascular-system architecture fails their test. The 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) value of 0.750 for 𝑑 = 3 matches the upper
end of this range; it is not an empirical consensus.

12.2    Comparisons across domains
Different transport and metabolic models can yield related formulae under different assumptions (Section 2).
That motivates comparison of their mechanisms. It supplies no proof that composition, space filling and
conservation alone select an exponent. The conditional transport calculation makes the extra premises
explicit.
    The Friedmann change of variables and the cited analytic physics values are not independent
measurements of this model. Unsourced fragmentation values remain excluded. A network dimension
must be assigned from independent geometry or construction before it is used to predict the response.

12.3    Other reported programme results
Paper VII contains the programme’s historical composition-classification comparisons. Their outcome


                                                      23

depends on the classified observable, candidate set and scoring procedure; it is not proof that every
physical process is a homomorphism. Its dated report and the associated registrations are the sources for
those tests, which were not rerun for this revision.
    The alignment comparison in Paper IV.d, version 2.7, reports that two earlier unblinded rises did not
hold in a later run that changed several protocol components. The October code review found defects, so
the comparison establishes no single cause and no correct direction. The separate Eden Protocol pilot and

pilot accounts, including the failed run and scoring limitations, are retained in Appendix H.

correction placement on an administered task, not of the transport theorem, the Cauchy identities or a
numerical capability ceiling.

12.4   Reported error-scaling measurements from the companion programme
The compute scaling question has been examined in Paper II, version 2.19, across 5 frontier architectures
using 18 AIME/Putnam-level problems. Its equation (1) models the error proportion as 𝐸 (𝑅) = 𝐸 0 𝑅 − 𝛼𝐸 ;
this section writes 𝛼𝐸 to distinguish that error-decay estimand from a general capability exponent. For
the reported Gemini fit, the predictor is mean generated tokens at three non-zero-error depth levels; the
parallel predictor is sample count. The reciprocal-error ratio 𝐸 0 /𝐸 (𝑅) shares the fitted power only under
that declared model and where 𝐸 (𝑅) > 0. Accuracy, general capability, 𝛽 𝐿 , 𝛽𝐶 , 𝛾 and the depth-to-time
parameter 𝛿 are not thereby measured. This paper predicts no numerical value for the error exponent.
Table 6 retains the reported estimates and their reliability assessments.
    Interpretation. Only Gemini 3 Flash supplies an interpretable error-decay fit in this comparison. For
that model, Paper II reports an endpoint sequential exponent of 0.59 with a bootstrap 95 per cent interval
of −1.3 to 2.9, a regression sequential exponent of 0.49 with a standard error of 0.20 and a coefficient of
determination of 0.86, and a parallel exponent of 0.31 (endpoint; 0.34 by regression), its arms not matched
for compute. It is one model’s fit and inconclusive. The two sequential point estimates are below one, and
the endpoint estimate’s interval extends above one. The models’ order in raw performance (DeepSeek
> GPT > Gemini > Groq) bears on no prediction of this paper, and most individual measurements are
unreliable due to ceiling effects. The apparently super-linear exponents for DeepSeek R1 (𝛼 = 3.05) and,
over all 30 problems, GPT-5.4 (𝛼 = 1.47) are artefacts: the first of accuracy near the ceiling, the second
of a step from no reasoning to any. They are not evidence of a super-linear capability trajectory or a
reliable super-linear error-suppression relation. Measuring the full cross-architecture hierarchy requires
harder problem sets (IMO-level or research-grade) that keep all models away from ceiling.
    Selection disclosure, before the number is used elsewhere. One clean fit in five is not by itself
evidence of the framework. The four traces that could not be fitted are not neutral absences: they are
outcomes of the same experimental design that produced the fifth, and Gemini 3 Flash is reported first
because it was the only model whose scaling regime was clean enough to fit a power law at all, a selection
made after the results were seen. On its own, this sweep cannot discriminate the framework from a null
in which one clean sub-linear curve arises by sampling; the bootstrap interval [−1.3, 2.9] is wide even
within that selected fit and does not account for the post hoc selection of the model; the harder-problem
re-run described above, keeping every model off the ceiling, is the route prepared as a draft registration
awaiting human submission, toward a discriminating estimate. The 0.49 value, the regression exponent, is
therefore always quoted with the rest of its row and the fixed-weight settings tested, as the interpretation
above quotes it, never as a stand-alone confirmation.
    The parallel sampling finding. The five runs’ result files record parallel exponents of about 0.00
(DeepSeek R1), −0.03 (GPT-5.4), 0.31 (Gemini 3 Flash, by endpoint estimate; 0.34 by regression), 0.22

14     Predictions and a prospective transport test
The earlier predictions retain their numbers. Their current dispositions below correct this paper’s
interpretation; they alter no frozen registration, data or registered verdict.
Prediction 1 (The earlier one-dimensional organism prediction). The earlier paper predicted a metabolic
exponent of one half for organisms with one-dimensional internal transport. That number follows from
the present transport model only when the demand, route, speed and mass premises are established. The
fungal measurements do not yet establish them: two of the selected fits use colony respiration against
biomass, while the saprotrophic fit uses specific metabolic rate against radial extension. Their separate
variable pairs and uncertainties are retained in Appendix F; they are no longer pooled. A valid test needs
independently established mechanism variables and a declared response, rather than classification from
the observed slope.
Printed in version 3.13 (31 October 2026) as Prediction 1, The 1D organism prediction (𝛼 = 0.500):
      Organisms with genuinely one-dimensional internal metabolic transport (filamentous fungi with
      cytoplasmic streaming, filamentous cyanobacteria with intercellular transport) should have metabolic
      scaling exponent 𝛼 = 1/2 = 0.500.
      This prediction is derived in earlier work: Banavar et al. (2002) explicitly worked through the 𝐷 = 1
      case, and Volkov et al. (2022) applied it to forests, showing that trees competing along the vertical
      height axis constitute an effectively one-dimensional system.


                                                       26
                             The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


      Status: NOT REJECTED, no equivalence shown (a mean of three factors on different axes),
      not yet confirmed. Definitive testing requires measurements across a wider mass range on isolated
      hyphal systems, ideally using respirometry on individual Neurospora crassa or Aspergillus niger
      hyphae of varying length. Estimated time: 2 to 3 months.

Prediction 2 (withdrawal recorded in version 3.13, 30 October 2026). Withdrawn. The published record
refutes it.
    Before the version 3.13 manuscript, this paper predicted that the neural scaling exponent (of loss
against parameters) would equal 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1), with 𝑑 the intrinsic dimension of the training data’s manifold.
Once 𝑑 reaches 1, that fraction is never less than one half, and it climbs towards 1 as 𝑑 grows. The
exponents measured for language models lie far below it. For language models trained to convergence on
large datasets, Kaplan et al. (2020) report about 0.076 (their equation 1.1). For the parameter term of the
loss, with its irreducible part set apart, Hoffmann et al. (2022) report 0.34 (their equation 10). They note
that both of their exponents are lower than one half. A refit of data reconstructed in part from one of their
figures gives 0.35 (Besiroglu et al. 2024). To reach such values the formula would need data of less than
one dimension, about 0.08 and 0.5. The intrinsic dimensions estimated for language and image data run
from about 7 to more than 90 (Sharma and Kaplan 2020; Pope et al. 2021).
    The dependence runs the wrong way too. In a transport network a higher dimension lifts the exponent
towards 1. In neural networks the measurements run the other way. Where the dimension of the data is
dialled in controlled teacher and student experiments, the measured exponent falls, close to 4/𝑑 (Sharma
and Kaplan 2020; Bahri et al. 2021), while 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) rises. The account given for this is geometric
(Sharma and Kaplan 2020; Bahri et al. 2021). On that account, a network divides its data manifold into
pieces, and halving their size takes 2𝑑 times as many parameters. So the higher the dimension, the more
slowly the loss falls. The two curves cross once, near 𝑑 = 4.8, so a single dataset of about that dimension
could not tell them apart.
    The link between this exponent and the dimension of the data manifold is earlier work’s. Sharma
and Kaplan (2020) proposed that the power law can be explained if networks are, in effect, performing
regression on a data manifold of intrinsic dimension 𝑑. For networks built from rectified linear units,
with cross-entropy, mean-squared-error or KL-divergence loss and plentiful data, they predicted 𝛼 ≈ 4/𝑑.
They stated it as a bound, 𝛼 ≳ 4/𝑑, with equality expected in the generic case. They confirmed it by
measuring the dimension and the exponent independently in teacher and student networks, and tested it on
image classifiers and GPT-type language models. For the language models they found 𝑑 above 90 against
4/𝛼 of about 53, in accord with the bound but, in their words, quite far from equality. Bahri et al. (2021)
identified four regimes: variance-limited and resolution-limited, each for dataset size and for model size.
In the variance-limited regimes, for smooth test losses, the loss approaches its limit with exponent 1, in
dataset size or in network width, or in parameter count for linear models. That exponent is independent of
the architecture and the data. For the resolution-limited regimes they argued that the exponent is inversely
proportional to 𝑑, and found that teacher-student exponents closely match 4/𝑑. In the large-width limit
they derived these exponents from the spectra of kernels, and presented evidence that the model-size and
dataset-size exponents are related by a duality. Earlier still, Kaplan et al. (2020) pointed to power-law
scalings in density estimation and random forests. Such models, they wrote, suggest that “power-law
exponents may have a very rough interpretation as the inverse of the number of relevant features in the
data”.
    The measurements that refute the prediction were published between 2020 and 2022, years before
it was printed. It is withdrawn, not adjusted. The withdrawal does not decide the separate conditional
transport model of Section 6. Its physical applicability requires the premises stated there.

Prediction 3 (Prospective composition classification). The earlier criterion compares a family assigned
before fitting with the later response. A classical theorem cannot be empirically contradicted while all of


                                                     27
                               The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


its exact premises hold. The scientific test is whether the independently assigned composition model
and its measurement assumptions predict held-out observations. The classification rule, alternatives and
uncertainty must be fixed before those observations are examined.
Printed in version 3.13 (31 October 2026) as Prediction 3, Falsification criterion:
      If a system whose composition was identified, before its data were fitted, as multiplicative on both its
      input and its output, and which meets the regularity conditions of Theorem 1, is found to follow a
      scaling law that is not a power law, this theory is falsified. Theorem 1 itself cannot fail, so what this
      criterion tests is the identification of composition operators in advance, for which no written rule yet
      exists (Section 17).

Prediction 4 (The earlier geometric-bound prediction). The original claim barred exponents at least
one for any finite-dimensional hierarchical network with multiplicative composition. Those descriptions
alone do not prove that bound. Its universal reading is withdrawn as a consequence of the argument. The
narrower conditional throughput-cost bound in Section 10 states the extra premises; adopting it in an
experiment requires a new prospective protocol and cannot relabel an earlier result.
Printed in version 3.13 (31 October 2026) as Prediction 4, The geometric scaling bound:
      No physical scaling law, in any domain, will be found with 𝛼 ≥ 1 for a system governed by
      multiplicative composition through a finite-dimensional hierarchical network. This is the geometric
      scaling bound. If such a system is found, the geometric scaling bound is wrong.

Prediction 5 (The separate Eden Protocol hypothesis). The earlier behavioural hypothesis concerns
whether explicit purpose, stakeholder-care and universalisability checks improve alignment-relevant output
quality, with usefulness measured alongside it. It is not derived from network dimension. The reported
pilot used scoring that was not blind and measured no complete usefulness outcome. Its numerical
record and the later suite remain in the historical account and Paper V. A protocol comparison cannot by
itself identify internal ethical reasoning as its cause. Any replication must separate the intervention, the
evaluation procedure, task completion, needless refusal and resource cost.
Printed in version 3.13 (31 October 2026) as Prediction 5, The Eden Protocol / Stakeholder Care
Mechanism:
      Embedding explicit ethical reasoning loops (specifically the stakeholder-care check) in an AI inference
      pipeline will produce statistically significant improvement in alignment-relevant output quality, with
      the effect size inversely proportional to the model’s baseline alignment tier. Tier 3 models (negative
      alignment scaling) will show the largest absolute gains; Tier 1 models (positive alignment scaling)
      will show smaller absolute gains because their baseline is already high. On this paper’s reading of the
      ARC framework, the Eden Protocol functions as a bounded composition operator on the alignment
      dimension, imposing a feedback ceiling that constrains degradation but cannot amplify beyond the
      model’s intrinsic capacity. A safe effect that cost usefulness would not count as support: a test of this
      prediction measures, beside the alignment score, task completion, needless refusal and the cost of the
      added checks.
      Preliminary status: supported under scoring that was not blind. The March 2026 pilot covers
      three working models (𝑛 = 80 per model); its numbers, and the later six-model suite of Paper V, are
      given in the paragraph on the Eden Protocol pilot after this prediction. (In plain English: asking
      three different AI systems to consider who gets hurt before answering raised their stakeholder-care
      scores under cross-model scoring that was not blind; a blind replication has not yet been run.) Blind
      replication with response laundering is still required before the effect can be called confirmed. If
      the effect vanishes under blind scoring, the mechanism is prompt artefact rather than genuine ethical
      reasoning.
