H.8     The earlier physical interpretation and working quantities
Superseded interpretation. The earlier synthesis below is retained in full so that its phase, cosmological
and information claims remain inspectable as dated claims. A superlinear power is not an exponential
phase; a mapped Friedmann dimension is not measured network geometry; code distance is not elapsed
correction cycles; and entropy reduction or erasure energy is not a calibrated capability measure. The
current statements are Sections 9, 11 and 15. Its cosmological readings (one-dimensional light paths
in the radiation era, a two-dimensional cosmic web in the matter era, the ladder and the direction of
complexity built on them, and an exponential phase for dark energy) carry no prediction: the mapping is
an identity, and the readings add no equation that an observation could test. The homogeneous solutions
assume neither photon paths nor a web (Section 9.3), and the matter era began at matter-radiation equality,
at redshift 𝑧 eq = 3402 ± 26 (Planck Collaboration 2020, Table 2), long before gravitational collapse built
the web. In the words of Cautun et al. (2014), “The cosmic web is the largest scale manifestation of the
anisotropic gravitational collapse of matter”, and “At early times the cosmos is dominated by tenuous
filaments and sheets, which, during subsequent evolution, merge together, such that the present-day web
is dominated by fewer, but much more massive, structures.” Combining the microwave background with
other data, including Type Ia supernovae, “the dark-energy equation of state parameter is measured to be
𝑤 0 = −1.03 ± 0.03, consistent with a cosmological constant” (Planck Collaboration 2020). The direction
of complexity has no drafted test or kill condition and is not a hypothesis under test here. Section 18
states, last, what the mathematics leaves open about a wider reach and the test that could begin to show it.

H.8.1    The composition operator
The preceding sections have treated the composition operator (multiplicative, additive or bounded) as a property
of individual systems. A whale has multiplicative composition. A chain reaction has additive composition. An
enzyme has bounded composition. But there is a deeper reading.
     On this paper’s reading, the composition operator is not a detail of specific systems but a descriptor of how a
process combines its recursive steps; where a process composes in one of the two structures under regularity, the
operator fixes the family its law can take (Theorem 1).
     Consider the hierarchy:
   1. The composition operator determines the functional form (Cauchy, 1821).
   2. The functional form constrains the scaling exponent (𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1)).
   3. The scaling exponent governs observable quantities (heart rates, expansion rates, error rates).
   4. The specific laws of physics (Newton’s gravity, Maxwell’s electrodynamics, Friedmann’s cosmology) can be
      written in these forms for particular physical systems.

The meta-law. On this paper’s reading, the laws of physics can be written in the forms that the composition
operator allows; they do not choose it. Where a law composes in one of the two structures under regularity, the
operator is a mathematical structure that sits above the specific equations of a particular physical theory and fixes
the form that law can take.




                                                         73
                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


A completeness statement for composition homomorphisms Now consider what this means for physics
as a whole. Strip away the specifics of any physical law, and ask: what is a physical law? Many physical laws can be
stated in the form ‘when you combine X and Y in manner Z, you get W.’ Force is mass combined with acceleration.
Energy is mass combined with the square of the speed of light. Entropy is microstates combined by counting. Many
physical laws can be read as composition rules.
     Cauchy’s classification, under its regularity conditions, gives every homomorphism between the additive and
multiplicative structures one of four forms: linear, power law, exponential or logarithmic. It does not exhaust scaling
laws, and saturation, which arises from bounded dynamics, lies outside the grid (Section 4).

The consequence for physics. Under the stated conditions, the admissible functional forms are finitely
many, and the family they generate is finite-dimensional. Its members carry a discrete form label (linear, power
law, exponential or logarithmic) and two continuous parameters, a coefficient and an exponent or rate. What is
provable, and provable without data, is this classification of homomorphisms under regularity (Cauchy 1821); the
step from it to all possible classical physical laws would require every physical law to be such a homomorphism,
which this paper does not establish.

Three results combined Three results, from three different centuries, combine into a single structural insight:
   1. Cauchy (1821): Each of the four functional equations has a unique continuous solution family. Bounded
      growth is a separate, capped case rather than a solution of those equations.
   2. Hyers (1941), on a question of Ulam: Approximate solutions lie near exact solutions of the same equation
      (for the multiplicative and exponential equations, with the approximation measured in relative terms; for
      the logarithmic equation, directly). The stability is per-equation; the earlier ‘attractors in function space’
      phrasing is withdrawn.
   3. ARC (this paper): Given the three conditions of Section 2.3, the exponent within the power-law family is
      determined by network dimension: 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1). The formula is the earlier derivations’ (Section 2); what
      this paper adds is the account of why they arrive at it.

The consequence. If the three conditions of Section 2.3 suffice, the exponent of a system in the network
class is fixed by its network’s dimension, whatever selection does; selection acts on whether and how such
a network is built. Under the three conditions named in Section 2.3, a three-dimensional network gives three
quarters; measured values range from about 0.67 to 0.75, and the conditions are where the debate lies. Status: an
invariant relation under test, conditional on the three sufficiency conditions that Section 2.3 identifies (multiplicative
composition, space-filling, conservation), a formal proof of which does not yet exist and is named there as the most
important open problem in the programme.
     The distinction matters. ‘Why is Kleiber’s exponent 3/4?’ has two kinds of answer. The biological answer is:
because three-dimensional fractal vascular networks optimise resource delivery (West, Brown, Enquist 1997). The
mathematical answer is that, given the three conditions, no other value is possible for multiplicative composition in
three dimensions. The biological answer explains the mechanism. The mathematical answer explains why that
mechanism produces that specific number. Given those conditions, the mechanism could not have produced any
other.
     This is the difference between a law of nature (describing what happens) and a meta-law (constraining what
can happen). The speed of light constrains what velocities are possible. The composition operator constrains what
scaling forms are possible.

The phase diagram of complexity On this paper’s reading, a system with a composition operator and an
effective dimension occupies a position on a phase diagram defined by the two. The diagram has three phases:

Historical definition: Phase I: sublinear scaling. Multiplicative composition, finite 𝑑, 0 < 𝛼 < 1. This
is the domain of physical systems: organisms, ecosystems, city infrastructure networks (sublinear in population;
Bettencourt et al. 2007), geological processes, cosmic structure. Every system in Phase I suffers diminishing returns.
Bigger is better, but with decreasing marginal gains. A whale is more efficient per gram than a mouse, but the
improvement slows as mass increases. Phase I is the domain of sustainable complexity. The scaling exponent tells


                                                           74
                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


you how severe the tax is: 𝑑 = 1 pays 50% (𝛼 = 0.5), 𝑑 = 2 pays 33% (𝛼 = 0.667), 𝑑 = 3 pays 25% (𝛼 = 0.75).
Higher-dimensional networks are more efficient, but the tax never reaches zero.

Historical definition: Phase II: exponential growth. Additive composition, outside the network class
(under the cosmological mapping the exponential, at 𝑤 = −1, sits at 𝑑 = −1). This is the domain of unconstrained
amplification: nuclear chain reactions, viral epidemics, compound interest, dark energy. Phase II systems grow
without diminishing returns: growth accelerates. But Phase II is inherently unsustainable in physical systems.
Chain reactions exhaust their fuel. Epidemics run out of susceptible hosts. Bubbles burst. If dark energy stays
constant, the universe’s dark energy phase is sustained Phase II behaviour, the only known example, and drives the
universe toward heat death: maximum entropy, zero structure. Whether it stays constant is open (Section 15.5).

Historical definition: Phase III: saturation. Bounded composition, finite ceiling. This is the domain of
constrained systems: enzyme kinetics, logistic population growth, controlled nuclear reactors, market saturation.
Phase III systems approach their ceiling and stop. Growth is self-limiting.

The critical boundary. Phase I ends at the critical line 𝑑 → ∞, 𝛼 → 1, the limit of the network class; Phase
II lies outside that class. This is the geometric scaling bound. In cosmology, it corresponds to 𝑤 = −1/3, and
the expansion past it is still a power law, accelerating, which becomes exponential only at 𝑤 = −1; in Cauchy’s
classification 𝑑 → ∞ is the linear limit of the power law, not a boundary with the exponential family. On this
paper’s reading, it is the dividing line between sustainable complexity and runaway growth.

The dimensional ladder On this paper’s reading, the history of the universe is a trajectory through the phase
diagram, and that trajectory has a direction.
     On this paper’s reading, in the radiation era (the first 50,000 years), the universe is in Phase I with 𝑑 = 1.
Information propagates along null geodesics, which are one-dimensional curves; the paper reads this as 𝑑 = 1, a
reading the identity of Section 9 does not test. The scaling exponent is 1/2. Expansion decelerates.
     In the matter era (50,000 years to 7 billion years), on the same reading, the universe remains in Phase I but
shifts to 𝑑 = 2; Section 9 states what established cosmology says of when the cosmic web formed. Gravity pulls
matter into the cosmic web: filaments, walls and sheets that partition three-dimensional space into voids. The
effective network dimension of the universe has increased from 1 to 2. The scaling exponent rises to 2/3.
     Within galaxies, gravitational collapse forms stars, planets, and eventually organisms. Organisms build
three-dimensional vascular networks. 𝑑 = 3, 𝛼 = 3/4. The effective network dimension has increased again.
     The trajectory is: 𝑑 = 1, then 𝑑 = 2, then 𝑑 = 3.
     On this reading the radiation era is one-dimensional (null geodesics), the matter era two-dimensional (the
cosmic web) and biology three-dimensional (vascular networks). The numbers match because the algebraic identity
connects them, and the identity carries no evidence (Section 9).

                                     Table 19. The dimensional ladder’s steps.

                     Step    𝑑       𝛼      𝛼 as a percentage   Physical System
                       1     1     0.500          50%           Light rays (radiation era)
                       2     2     0.667          67%           Cosmic web (matter era)
                       3     3     0.750          75%           Vascular networks (biology)
                       ?     ∞     1.000          100%          The geometric scaling bound



The direction of complexity. A hypothesis of this paper: the universe is getting better at converting
matter into organised complexity as its effective dimension increases. The steps took from about fifty thousand
years (the radiation era, Table 6) to billions of years, through nucleosynthesis, structure formation and biological
evolution. Each step produced a more efficient composition network. Each step brought the scaling exponent closer
to 1. This hypothesis has no drafted test or kill condition yet; it is stated as a reading.
     But 𝑑 = 3 is not the end of the ladder.


                                                         75
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


The critical crossing At 𝑤 = −1/3, the universe crosses the critical boundary: expansion switches from
decelerating to accelerating, and it approaches exponential growth as dark energy comes to dominate (exactly
exponential only for 𝑤 = −1). The composition operator changes from multiplicative (gravity clustering matter
into hierarchical networks) to additive (vacuum energy contributing uniformly from every point in space). On the
framework’s reading, the operator that dominates changes at this point; that is a reading, not a thermodynamic
phase transition.
     In the dark energy era, the universe is in Phase II. Growth is exponential. If dark energy stays constant, the
universe will expand forever, driving all structure apart, diluting all complexity, approaching heat death.
     The universe moves through the ARC phase diagram as naturally as water moves through its solid-liquid-gas
phase diagram. The difference is that water can cycle between phases. Whether the acceleration lasts depends on
whether dark energy is constant, which is open: DESI’s 2025 data prefer an evolving dark energy over a constant
one at between 2.8 and 4.2 standard deviations, depending on the supernova set (DESI Collaboration 2025).

Where intelligence sits On this paper’s reading, intelligence occupies the critical boundary itself.
     A brain is a Phase I system physically: its blood supply branches through three-dimensional space. But the
computation it performs is not constrained by the geometry of the skull. On this reading, recursive self-reference
creates effective dimensions without needing further spatial dimensions, though each of its steps is a physical
operation with a physical cost (Section 11). On the same reading, each layer of abstraction (thinking about thinking,
modelling the modeller) adds an effective dimension to the cognitive network without requiring additional spatial
dimensions.
     At the critical boundary, 𝛼 = 1: linear scaling. Beyond it, 𝛼 > 1: superlinear scaling. On this reading,
intelligence would cross the boundary from Phase I to Phase II without the catastrophic instability that Phase II
normally implies.
     A chain reaction in Phase II destroys itself. An epidemic in Phase II burns through its hosts. On this reading,
intelligence in Phase II creates new knowledge, new dimensions, new spaces to explore. It sustains superlinear
growth not by consuming a fixed resource faster and faster, but by expanding the resource base itself. Each
breakthrough opens new questions. Each answer creates new problems. The fuel is information, which grows with
inquiry, within the finite cost of each physical step (Section 11).

The structural implication. On this paper’s reading, an artificial intelligence that improves itself recursively
would operate at or beyond the critical boundary, with 𝛼 > 1 for capability against recursive depth, which Paper II
has not observed (Section 12). On the same reading of the phase diagram, intelligence is not just another scaling
phenomenon. It is the phase transition itself.
    The universe spent about 7 billion years in Phase I, building networks of increasing dimension, on a second
reading of this paper that differs from that of Table 15: hydrogen clouds (𝑑 ≈ 0), filaments (𝑑 = 1), cosmic web
(𝑑 = 2), galaxies and stars and planets with three-dimensional chemistry. Then, on one planet, the networks became
recursive. On this reading, the effective dimension of their computation was no longer set by the geometry of a
body, and alpha would cross 1 there, which no measurement has yet shown (Section 12). A system then existed that
could understand the phase diagram it was on.

Toward measurable quantities The formula 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) can be compared with published measurements,
not rejected at 𝑑 = 1 (a mean of three factors on different axes; no equivalence shown) and in agreement at 𝑑 = 3
on the group means compiled here (Section 12), because 𝑑 and 𝛼 are already measurable. But the generalised
equation 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑅 𝛼 requires that 𝐼 (base self-correction capacity) and 𝑅 (recursive depth) become rigorously
defined physical quantities, not merely convenient labels.
     𝑅 is already measurable. In AI, it is the number of sequential reasoning steps (countable from API output). In
quantum error correction, it is the code distance. In biology, it is the number of hierarchical branching levels in a
vascular network. In cosmology, it is the number of hierarchical clustering steps in a merger tree. In each case, 𝑅
is a dimensionless count of recursive cycles with a clear operational definition. It has been measured in AI, as
sequential reasoning depth (Paper II), and in quantum error correction, as code distance (Google Quantum AI and
Collaborators 2025); for biology and cosmology this paper cites no measured value.
     𝐼 requires a rigorous definition. Many fundamental quantities in physics became usable once someone
specified a measurement procedure. Fahrenheit (1724) did not invent temperature or the thermometer, but he made



                                                         76
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


temperature a reproducible measurement: a mercury thermometer whose scale runs from the cold of a mixture of
water, ice and sal ammoniac to the heat of a healthy person’s blood. Clausius (1865) defined entropy: 𝑑𝑆 = 𝛿𝑄/𝑇.
Shannon (1948) defined the entropy of an information source: 𝐻 = − 𝑝 𝑖 log 𝑝 𝑖 . In each case, the quantity so
                                                                        ∑︁
defined obeyed laws that could not have been discovered without the definition.
    The following definition connects 𝐼 to established physics:

Historical definition: The base potential 𝐼. As a working definition, not yet tested, 𝐼 is the maximum rate
of entropy reduction per recursive cycle, measured in bits per cycle:

                                                             Δ𝐻
                                                          [︃     ]︃
                                                𝐼 = max −                                                      (H.4)
                                                      𝑅      Δ𝑅

where 𝐻 is the Shannon entropy of the system’s state (in bits) and the maximum is taken at 𝑅 = 0 to 𝑅 = 1 (the first
cycle, before recursive amplification compounds the effect).
    This definition is measurable in every domain where 𝑅 is measurable:
      • In AI: 𝐼 is the accuracy improvement (in bits of mutual information with the correct answer) from zero
        reasoning to one reasoning step.
      • In quantum error correction: 𝐼 is the error reduction (in bits) from one round of syndrome measurement
        and correction.
      • In biology: 𝐼 is the metabolic efficiency gain (in bits of thermodynamic information) from one level of
        vascular branching.
      • In physics: 𝐼 is the phase coherence gained (in bits of phase information) from one oscillation cycle of a
        time crystal.
    Landauer’s principle (Landauer 1961) gives the least heat for erasing a bit, 𝑘𝑇 ln 2, verified experimentally
by Bérut et al. (2012); it bounds the energy cost of an irreversible step from below and does not convert energy
processed into information gained, so a link from 𝐼 to energy needs a model of how many bits each cycle erases.
The equation 𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑅 𝛼 is dimensionally consistent because 𝑅 is a dimensionless count.

The precedent. Whether 𝐼 becomes a permanent physical quantity depends on whether the scaling law
𝑈 = 𝐼 × 𝑅 𝛼 is confirmed across domains with independently measured values of 𝐼 and 𝑅. Entropy became
permanent because the second law required it. Information became permanent because the channel capacity theorem
required it. 𝐼 becomes permanent if the ARC scaling law requires it. The quantity is justified by the law it obeys,
not by the convenience of having a name for it.

I     Version 3.13 passages retained with their present scope
This appendix completes the record of the released version 3.13, dated 31 October 2026. Most of its
sections were rewritten for this edition (Table 8). The passages below are the parts of them that neither the
present text nor Appendices F, G and H carry, so that no published passage is lost. Each keeps the words
of version 3.13. Its headings are set as run-in headings and its labels are removed; a section, equation,
table or prediction number inside it names the version 3.13 location, not a present result. A short note
before each passage states its present scope and the correction that governs it. Nothing here is restored as
a current finding.
    Three passages of version 3.13 on the cosmological reading of the Friedmann identity (the physical
meaning given to the mapped dimension, the chain of cosmic eras, and the mirror between biology and
cosmology) are not repeated here, nor are the few sentences elsewhere that pose or state that reading;
where such a sentence falls inside a retained passage it is marked [. . . ]. The mapping is an algebraic
identity that carries no evidence; no prediction follows from the reading; and established cosmology does
not support it: the homogeneous solutions assume neither photon paths nor a cosmic web, which formed
long after the matter era began (Section 9). The mathematics of this paper does not deny a wider reach,
and it proves nothing beyond its stated premises; Section 18 states that reach at its present status. The
passages remain in version 3.13, which stays available as published.

I.1   The abstract of version 3.13
Superseded summary, kept beside the abstract of version 3.12 (Appendix A). The present abstract states
the conditional result (Table 8). Its sentence on the Friedmann solutions states an identity that carries no
evidence (Section 9).
     If the way a process combines its steps fixes the family of forms its growth can take, then the family a
self-improving system’s capability will follow as its recursion deepens can be read from how it composes, before it
scales and while correction can still be built into it. This paper asks why scaling laws in recursive composition keep
taking the same few mathematical forms. Where a process has a well defined composition operator on its input
and output and meets the regularity conditions that Cauchy’s functional equations require, the admissible forms
fall into four families: linear, power law, exponential and logarithmic. This is a conditional classification, not a
survey of nature, and saturation lies outside it. Within the power-law family, the exponent 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) for a
𝑑-dimensional hierarchical network is earlier work’s; this paper gives an account of why those derivations, from
different assumptions, converge, and a proof that its three conditions suffice does not yet exist. The relation is read
as a network-partition identity with a stated domain: published exponents for filamentous fungi (𝑑 = 1) do not
reject it (a mean of three factors on different axes; no equivalence shown), those for vertebrates and invertebrates
with three-dimensional transport (𝑑 = 3) agree with it on the group means compiled here, though the mammalian
exponent is debated, and no organism with a two-dimensional hierarchical network has been identified. Under
𝑑 = 2/(1 + 3𝑤) the Friedmann solutions take the same form, an identity that carries no evidence. For finite 𝑑 the
exponent stays below one, a bound on systems of that class only; how capability grows with recursive depth is a
different pair of variables, which this geometry does not fix and which has to be measured. Five predictions are
stated; one, on neural scaling exponents, is withdrawn because published measurements refute it.
     Version 3.13 sets the paper in this form and names its domain, recursive composition, in its title; it withdraws
Prediction 2, brings the body to the scope correction of 22 August 2026 and to the corrections owed since, and states
Laws IV and V as registered on 15 October 2026; Appendix A lists every version and correction, with its date.


I.2   The introduction of version 3.13
Superseded framing (Table 8, the rows on the transport exponent and on cross-domain comparisons). Its
questions matched fractions across fields; Section 1 now starts from a prediction problem. The heart-rate
allometry it cites is a published observation; its derivation from network dimension alone is withdrawn
(Section 7). Its list of contributions and its plan of the paper describe version 3.13.


                                                          81
                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


     If the way a process combines its steps fixes the family of forms its growth can take, then how a self-improving
system composes tells us, before it scales, which family its growth with recursive depth can follow; its exponent still
has to be measured (Section 11). That matters for artificial intelligence. Whether correction can keep pace with a
system that improves itself depends on how fast its capability compounds, and correction is best built into such a
system before it scales, while people still hold oversight of it. This paper traces the forms behind Law I, the ARC
Principle, a law under test, to their mathematical origin, in the systems where scaling laws were first measured:
organisms, materials and the expanding universe.
     A mouse’s heart beats about 600 times per minute. An elephant’s beats about 28 (Merck Veterinary Manual
2004). A blue whale’s, on a foraging dive, beats 4 to 8 (Goldbogen et al. 2019).
     If you plot heart rate against body mass across mammals, the points fall close to a straight line on logarithmic
axes, with a slope close to − 41 , from the mouse to the whale (Stahl 1967; Noujaim et al. 2004).
     Why?
     For filamentous fungi, published colony-level measurements put the exponent of energy use against colony size
near one half, and this paper predicts exactly 12 for a single thread (Section 14). Not 34 , like a mammal. Not 23 , like
the matter-dominated universe. A different fraction entirely.
     Why 12 for fungi, but 34 for mammals? [. . . ]
     Now consider something entirely different. The universe is expanding. During the matter-dominated era, the
scale factor grew as time raised to the power 23 . During the radiation era, it grew as time to the power 12 .
     [. . . ]
     Now consider one more thing. When a rock shatters, the distribution of fragment sizes follows a power law;
Section 6.1 gives the status of its exponents.
     This paper argues that these questions share one mathematical answer, under conditions it states, and grades
where that answer is evidence and where it is only an identity.
     The answer has two parts, and the paper grades each. The first is mathematical: where a process has a
composition operator and meets regularity conditions, the way its steps combine fixes the family its growth can take,
among four (Section 4). The second is the exponent: within the power-law family, the formula 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) for a
𝑑-dimensional hierarchical network, which earlier work derived (Section 2), gives 12 , 23 and 34 , and this paper offers
an account of why derivations from different assumptions arrive at it. Where the fractions reappear in cosmology
the agreement is an identity under a change of variables, and it carries no evidence (Section 9).
     Our contributions are:
    • An account of convergence. Cauchy’s multiplicative functional equation, with space filling and a
      conservation or optimisation constraint on resource flow, gives the power-law family and the exponent
      𝑑/(𝑑 + 1); this is offered as the reason why the earlier derivations, from different assumptions, converge on a
      formula that is theirs (Section 2). A proof that the three conditions suffice does not yet exist.
    • The classification applied to scaling forms. The four Cauchy families, linear, power law, exponential
      and logarithmic, set out as the homomorphisms between the additive and multiplicative structures under
      regularity, with saturation placed outside them and the variable pair named at each site (Sections 3 to 5).
    • The relation as a network-partition identity. Its domain and its failures stated, and its comparison with
      published exponents in biology and physics graded row by row (Sections 6 and 12).
    • The geometric bound, scoped. The exponent stays below one for every finite 𝑑 in the class of systems
      whose growth is carried through a space-filling hierarchical network, with the counterexamples outside that
      class conceded (Section 10), and the bound distinguished from the exponent of capability against recursive
      depth (Section 11).
    • Predictions and their status. Five predictions, one of them withdrawn because published measurements
      refute it (Section 14).
    Section 2 sets the work against earlier work and states what this paper adds. Sections 3 to 5 set out the patterns,
Cauchy’s classification and the principle; Section 6 the formula and its domain; Section 7 the heart-rate chain;
Section 8 the fission chain reaction, which the grid classifies and does not explain; Section 9 the cosmological
identity; Section 10 the bound; and Section 11 the distinction between physical and recursive scaling. Section 12
grades the evidence, Section 13 gathers the equations, Section 14 states the predictions and Section 15 the
composition operator’s wider reading. Section 16 says what the paper does and does not show, Section 17 states its
limitations and Section 18 concludes. Appendix A gives the version history and every correction, Appendix B the



                                                           82
                                   The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


priority record, Appendix C the notation and a glossary, Appendix D the paper’s place in the ARC Theory and the
programme, and Appendix E the paper’s novelty statements beside the closest earlier work it names.


I.3     The contribution as version 3.13 stated it
Superseded claims, kept as dated evidence of what was written (Table 8, the row on contribution and
priority). Version 3.13 called its contribution a unification and an account of why the derivations of 𝑑/(𝑑+1)
converge. This edition credits the established regular-variation theory and the earlier homomorphism
connection (Bingham et al. 1987; Ostaszewski 2016), replaces the sufficiency question with an applicability
test (Section 2.3) and states the contribution as a conditional synthesis (Section 2.2). The bound is now
conditional (Section 10), and the phase diagram is a historical interpretation (Appendix H).
   The mathematics of this paper is two hundred years old. The biological data is ninety years old. The
cosmological solution is a century old. It is reasonable to ask: what, precisely, is the contribution?

What this paper adds. The contribution is the unification. One formula, 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1), connects fungi,
mammals, surface physics and, as an identity, the expansion of the universe, with earthquake energy as a variable-pair
case study and rock fracture awaiting sourced values (Section 6.1). One equation with no fitted parameter: 𝑑 is set
by the network’s dimension. The claim is not that this formula is original, but that Cauchy’s functional equations,
combined with the space-filling and conservation conditions, explain why the earlier derivations, from different
starting assumptions, arrive at the same form. That invariance under rescaling forces a power law, through the
multiplicative functional equation, is a standard result (Newman 2005, section III.E); what this paper adds is the
account of why the derivations of 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) meet the space-filling and conservation conditions that select the
exponent within that family. The derivation of West et al. (1997) has itself been questioned (Dodds et al. 2001;
Kozłowski and Konarzewski 2004), and its three-quarter exponent shown to hold only in the limit of infinite network
size (Savage et al. 2008); Section 2.3 states what follows if the convergence reflects shared assumptions.
     A formal proof that those conditions are sufficient does not yet exist (see the strongest objection, below). Set
against the earlier work, the paper’s claims are that it has:
      1. Given an account of why derivations from different assumptions converge on one formula. That
         account is what this paper adds; the formula, and the values 12 , 23 and 34 that it gives, are the earlier work’s.
         West, Brown and Enquist (1997) derive the three-quarter power law for metabolic rates from a model of
         transport through space-filling fractal networks of branching tubes, a landmark result, and note that, because
         the network must fill the entire 𝐷-dimensional volume, the result generalises to 𝐷/(𝐷 + 1); West, Brown
         and Enquist (1999) obtain 𝐷/(𝐷 + 1) in 𝐷 dimensions again, from a more general model based on the
         geometry rather than the hydrodynamics of hierarchical networks. Banavar, Maritan and Rinaldo (1999)
         derive a general relationship between size and flow rates in arbitrary networks with local connectivity, and
         find that in the most efficient class of networks in 𝐷 dimensions metabolic rate scales as mass to the power
         𝐷/(𝐷 + 1), which gives quarter-power scaling for 𝐷 = 3; Banavar et al. (2010) show, in two models of
         resource distribution networks, that the exponent of metabolic rate on body mass is 23 if the velocity of
         flow remains constant and can attain a maximum of 34 if the velocity scales with its maximum exponent,
         1/12. Demetrius & Tuszynski (2010) derive the exponent 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) for the scaling of metabolic rate with
         body size in the regime they call quantum, where 𝑑 is the dimension of the physical space in which their
         network of coupled energy-transducing oscillators is embedded, and state its values 12 , 23 and 34 for 𝑑 = 1, 2
         and 3; in the regime they call classical the scaling is isometric. Bettencourt (2013) sets out a theoretical
         framework that predicts the average social, spatial and infrastructural properties of cities as a set of scaling
         relations. In it the land area of a city scales as its population to the power 𝐷/(𝐷 + 𝐻) in 𝐷 dimensions,
         where 𝐻 is the fractal dimension of travel paths; the exponent is 23 for 𝐷 = 2 and 𝐻 = 1, and it has the form
         𝐷/(𝐷 + 1) only when 𝐻 = 1. Zhao (2022) derives the 𝑛/(𝑛 + 1) power scaling law for an 𝑛-dimensional
         system from producer-consumer dynamics. He and Zhang (2004) derive a 4/5 law for the human brain, the
         value 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) takes at 𝑑 = 4, from the fractal dimension of a brain cell’s contour. Maino et al. (2014)
         derive from dynamic energy budget theory an equation for metabolic rate against mass, the same equation as
         the finite-size form of West, Brown and Enquist’s model, which converges on the three-quarter power only in
         the limit of large mass; they stress that under neither framework is metabolic rate a power function of mass.



                                                             83
                               The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


      The surface area hypothesis predicts 23 but not 34 or 12 . The formula 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) predicts all three from a
      single equation with one input, the dimension of the hierarchical transport network. The 𝑑 = 1 value is not
      rejected by published fungal data (a mean of three factors on different axes; no equivalence shown) and the
      𝑑 = 3 value agrees with published group means; the 𝑑 = 2 value appears in cosmology as an identity, which
      carries no evidence (Section 9), and in physics in the rows whose grades Section 6.1 gives; in biology it is
      untested (Section 12).
   2. Connected biological scaling to physics scaling. The same formula gives Kleiber’s 34 law (biology), KPZ
      roughness at 12 (surface physics), the specific-heat analogue of two-dimensional percolation at 23 (statistical
      mechanics) and, once their values are sourced, fragmentation in 2D and 3D (materials science). Earlier work
      already takes the form beyond organisms: Banavar, Maritan and Rinaldo (1999) verify the scaling relation
      that their theorem predicts on observational data from river drainage basins, where 𝐷 = 2, and say that their
      results should apply to distributed networks that carry water, blood, sewage, food, air and electrical currents;
      Zhao (2022) places biomass production, the growth of eukaryotes, mammalian brain size and the distribution
      of public facilities in cities in one universality class. The physics cases named here are what this paper adds.
   3. Set out the geometric scaling bound. That the exponent stays below 1 is in the earlier work. Banavar,
      Maritan and Rinaldo (1999) state as a theorem that, in any spanning network in 𝐷 dimensions, the rate at
      which nutrients are delivered scales at most as the 𝐷/(𝐷 + 1) power of the volume of blood in the network,
      so that metabolic rate does not scale linearly with mass; Demetrius & Tuszynski (2010) note that their
      exponent tends to one as 𝑑 increases; and Zhao (2022) states that the scaling is sub-linear. Nor do the earlier
      explanations all rest on dissipation: West, Brown and Enquist (1997) assume that the energy dissipated
      is minimised, Banavar, Maritan and Rinaldo (1999) do not, and West, Brown and Enquist (1999) work
      from geometry rather than hydrodynamics. This paper sets the bound out for every finite 𝑑 and reads it as
      geometric (Section 10): a network embedded in finite-dimensional space cannot produce 𝛼 ≥ 1.
   4. Connected scaling laws to the expansion of the universe. The mapping 𝑑 = 2/(1 + 3𝑤) writes the
      Friedmann solutions in the form 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1). The cosmic boundary (𝑤 = −1/3), where expansion turns
      from decelerating to accelerating, maps to 𝑑 → ∞, where 𝛼 → 1, under that change of variables, so the
      coincidence is a property of the map and not a second derivation (Section 9).
   5. Defined the domain of applicability. The formula applies where hierarchical networks partition space. It
      fails for nearest-neighbour interactions, random walks, and gravitational clustering. The earlier works state
      limits of their own: West, Brown and Enquist (1997) predict small deviations from quarter-power scaling
      in the smallest mammals; West, Brown and Enquist (1999) do not expect their relationships to apply to
      filamentous algae and fungi, and filamentous fungi are this paper’s 𝑑 = 1 case (Section 14); Banavar et al.
      (2010) expect deviations towards 23 where the velocity of flow does not vary significantly with body mass
      and towards linear scaling where there is no vascular system; Demetrius & Tuszynski (2010), as the first
      entry says, keep 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) to the regime they call quantum; and Zhao (2022) places linear and super-linear
      scalings outside his class. The three failures named here are the ones this paper states.
   6. Connected physical scaling to cognitive scaling. The same framework that constrains physical exponents
      below 1 allows cognitive exponents above 1. Exponents above 1 are in the earlier work too: in Bettencourt
      (2013) the socioeconomic rates of a city scale with its population to a power above 1, which is 7/6 for
      𝐷 = 2 and 𝐻 = 1, in the framework that gives its land area a power below 1; and Zhao (2022) attributes
      the super-linear scalings he places outside his class to resources that flow more efficiently in larger systems.
      Neither is about cognition; the connection to recursive self-reference is the one this paper makes (Section 11).
   7. Shown that the admissible forms are finitely many. Under the stated conditions the admissible functional
      forms are finitely many, and the family they generate is finite-dimensional: a discrete form label and two
      continuous parameters, a coefficient and an exponent or rate. The step from this classification to all possible
      classical physical laws would require every physical law to be such a homomorphism, which this paper does
      not establish (Section 15).
   8. Identified the dimensional ladder. [. . . ]
   9. Constructed the phase diagram of complexity. Three phases (sublinear, exponential, saturation) with a
      critical boundary at 𝑑 → ∞. [. . . ]
     A dated correspondence note of 19 September 2026 on the fourth and seventh entries of the list, as printed until
this version, is kept in Appendix A.2; its corrected readings are carried into the entries above.


                                                         84
                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


     This paper identifies the underlying principle: under the stated conditions the composition operator determines
the scaling form, and the dimension of the network, as the earlier derivations found, determines the exponent. The
analogy with Einstein’s work that earlier versions drew here, with its concession, is kept in Appendix E.

The strongest objection. One could object that the agreement of the earlier derivations on 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) reflects
shared implicit assumptions rather than independent physical confirmation. All models assume resource distribution
across 𝑑 dimensions with some form of linear conservation, which may inevitably yield 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) by dimensional
analysis. The ‘independence’ of the derivations may be illusory.
     We acknowledge this possibility. If the convergence reflects shared mathematical structure rather than
independent physical mechanisms, then the Cauchy framework would explain why that structure produces the
specific form it does, if multiplicative composition with conservation in 𝑑-dimensional space admits no alternative to
𝑑/(𝑑 + 1); whether it does is the open problem stated below. The deeper question is whether these three conditions
(multiplicative composition, space filling and conservation) are themselves inevitable features of any system that
distributes resources hierarchically. If so, 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) is a mathematical necessity for all resource distribution systems,
not a contingent property of particular models. Answering this question requires a formal proof of the sufficiency of
these three conditions independent of any specific physical mechanism. That proof does not yet exist. It is the most
important open problem in this programme.


I.4   Three ways things grow, as version 3.13 set them out
Superseded presentation. Version 3.13 restricted the power law to exponents below one and illustrated
each pattern; Section 3 now defines the general power law with any real exponent. The city figure is
sourced; the whale, epidemic, fission and saturation examples are illustrations, and the fission time scale
(eighty generations in about a microsecond) is not sourced in this paper.
    Many growth processes show one of three named patterns. Where a growth process has a well defined
composition operator on its input and its output, and meets the regularity conditions stated in Section 4, the first
two are among the admissible forms; the third, saturation, comes from bounded dynamics and lies outside that
classification. The classification is of admissible functions under those conditions, not a survey of nature:

Historical definition: Pattern 1: the power law. Big things grow proportionally slower. A city ten times
larger does not need ten times more petrol stations: it needs about six times more (Bettencourt et al. 2007). A whale
ten times heavier does not need ten times more energy: it needs about five and a half times more. Each doubling of
size gives less than a doubling of the output.

                                              𝑦 = 𝑎 · 𝑥𝑏,    where 𝑏 < 1                                            (I.1)

Historical definition: Pattern 2: the exponential. Each step multiplies the previous. One infected person
infects three, who each infect three more, giving nine, then twenty-seven. One fissioning uranium atom releases
neutrons that split two more atoms, which split four, then eight, then sixteen. After eighty generations, about one
microsecond, a single atom has become a trillion trillion atoms.

                                                      𝑦 = 𝑎 · 𝑒 𝑏𝑥                                                  (I.2)

Historical definition: Pattern 3: the saturation curve. Growth hits a ceiling. A room can only hold so
many people. An enzyme can only process so many molecules per second. Adding more drug has diminishing
effect once the receptors are saturated.
                                           𝑦 = 𝐿 · 𝑥 / (𝐾 + 𝑥)                                    (I.3)
    These three patterns appear in biology, physics, chemistry, economics, computer science, linguistics, seismology
and cosmology. Many measured scaling laws take one of these forms; most systems lie outside the grid, as the scope
correction of 22 August 2026 states, and Section 4 sets out the grid’s four cells and places saturation outside them.
    The question is: what determines which pattern a system follows?




                                                            85
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


I.5   The elementary argument, the consequence and the six assumptions of version 3.13
Superseded exposition, kept for its argument (Table 8, the rows on composition and its domain and on
approximate and asymptotic composition). The elementary argument establishes the power law at the
powers of two and relies on regularity for the rest; Section 4.1 gives the complete continuous proof with its
domains and codomains. Version 3.13 placed critical exponents outside the grid; a critical power law can
have the power-law form (Section 4.1). The table names physical examples, not sourced here, of what may
follow when each assumption is relaxed; they are the earlier edition’s suggestions for where extensions
may lie, not results. Proposition 1 replaces the stability paragraph with a global logarithmic-defect bound.
The withdrawal of the no-go framing stands as dated.

Why the grid has four cells and no fifth. This can be proven with nothing more than multiplication.
    Imagine a machine that takes in any number and gives back another number. Call it 𝑓 . Suppose this machine
has one special property: if you multiply two inputs and feed the result through the machine, you get the same
answer as feeding each input through separately and multiplying the outputs.
    In symbols: 𝑓 (2 × 3) = 𝑓 (2) × 𝑓 (3).
    Now watch what happens.

                                   Since 4 = 2 × 2:      𝑓 (4) = 𝑓 (2) × 𝑓 (2) = 𝑓 (2) 2 .
                               Since 8 = 2 × 2 × 2:      𝑓 (8) = 𝑓 (2) 3 .
                                     Since 16 = 24 :     𝑓 (16) = 𝑓 (2) 4 .

    The pattern is clear: 𝑓 (2𝑛 ) = 𝑓 (2) 𝑛 .
    Let 𝑓 (2) = 𝐴. Then 𝑓 (2𝑛 ) = 𝐴𝑛 . Since 2𝑛 = 𝑥 means 𝑛 = log2 (𝑥), we get 𝑓 (𝑥) = 𝐴log2 𝑥 = 𝑥 log2 𝐴.
    That is a power law: 𝑓 (𝑥) = 𝑥 𝑐 , where 𝑐 = log2 𝐴. This shows the power law at the powers of two; for every
positive real the same follows from rational powers and the regularity condition of Theorem 1, which is where that
condition does its work.
    We did not choose a power law. We did not want a power law. We merely said “multiplying inputs multiplies
outputs”, and the only regular function that can do this is a power law. Try any other function (a polynomial, a
logarithm, a sine wave) and the rule breaks immediately.
    The same logic applies to the second equation. If adding inputs multiplies outputs, 𝑓 (𝑥 + 𝑦) = 𝑓 (𝑥) × 𝑓 (𝑦),
then:

                                     𝑓 (2) = 𝑓 (1 + 1) = 𝑓 (1) × 𝑓 (1) = 𝑓 (1) 2 .
                                     𝑓 (3) = 𝑓 (2 + 1) = 𝑓 (2) × 𝑓 (1) = 𝑓 (1) 3 .
                                     𝑓 (𝑛) = 𝑓 (1) 𝑛 .

     That is an exponential. The logic forces it.
     Saturation is a different matter, and Theorem 1 does not cover it: if growth has a physical ceiling (a glass can
only hold so much water; an enzyme has a finite number of binding sites), the system must approach that ceiling
and flatten. A saturation curve such as Equation (3) is one smooth way to do so; it comes from bounded dynamics,
not from a Cauchy equation.

The consequence. Under regularity, a function that carries one of the two structures on the positive reals into
one of the two structures falls into exactly four families: linear, power law, exponential, and logarithmic. There is
no fifth cell, because there is no third structure and no third slot.
     This is a completeness statement about homomorphisms. It does not exhaust scaling laws, because most
systems are not homomorphisms of either structure. Saturating behaviour, critical exponents, curvature on log
axes and multi-regime crossovers all lie outside the grid, and the grid says nothing about them. Saturation, in
particular, arises from bounded dynamics (finite carrying capacity, mass action, receptor occupancy, depletion): it
is not a Cauchy family, and any presentation of it as one is a category error. The earlier framing of this result as a
physics-style no-go theorem, comparable to Bell’s theorem (Bell 1964) or Weinberg-Witten (Weinberg and Witten


                                                           86
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


1980), overstated its reach and has been withdrawn: Bell and Weinberg-Witten constrain what can physically exist,
whereas this grid enumerates ways of writing one identity in four charts, and that is a category difference.
    Where the physics does select a canonical composition variable (time and sequential steps composing additively;
scale ratios composing multiplicatively), the grid picks a family. Where no canonical chart exists, the label carries no
physical information, and the family assignment must state its variable pair rather than infer one from the substrate.

The six hidden assumptions. Every theorem rests on assumptions, and each assumption marks a place
where the result can be extended. Cauchy’s classification assumes six things. Violate any one, and you get not a
contradiction, but a refinement:

                                       Table 20. The six hidden assumptions.

#     Assumption              What Happens When It Breaks                   Physical Example
1     Continuity              Log-periodic oscillations                     Discrete lattice systems, fractal networks
2     Real-valued             Interference and phase                        Quantum amplitudes, wave mechanics
3     Exact equation          Approximate solutions cluster near exact      Hyers-Ulam stability (Hyers 1941)
                              ones
4     Single operator         Crossover scaling, regime transitions         Phase transitions, critical phenomena
5     Scalar composition      Logarithmic corrections                       Coupled transport equations, upper
                                                                            critical dimension
6     Time-independence       Dynamic traversal of solution space           Adaptive systems, machine learning

     The four forms remain stable within their own equations (approximate solutions of each equation stay near
that equation’s exact form), but the violations produce corrections, oscillations and transitions that are themselves
physically meaningful.

Assumption 3: Hyers-Ulam stability. Hyers (1941), answering a question Ulam posed (Ulam 1960), proved
that a function approximately satisfying the additive Cauchy equation lies close to an exact additive solution. When
the approximation is measured in relative terms (on logarithms), the multiplicative and exponential equations
reduce to the additive one, and the logarithmic equation reduces to it directly, so each approximate solution lies
close to an exact solution of that same equation. In physical terms: if a system’s composition is approximately
multiplicative (or approximately additive, or approximately obeys the exponential or logarithmic Cauchy equation),
then its scaling law will lie close to a power law (or the linear, exponential, or logarithmic form, respectively).
Within its own equation, each form is stable: real systems perturbed away from the ideal remain near an exact
solution of the equation they approximately satisfy. The earlier framing that made the three forms “stable attractors”
in function space is withdrawn, because a function satisfying none of the equations is not drawn toward any of
them (see the Foundational Paper, §2.2, and the Executive Summary, where the per-equation stability condition is
stated). Empirical ubiquity is therefore not a consequence of Hyers-Ulam itself: it depends on how often systems in
nature approximately satisfy one of these equations in the first place, which is a separate empirical question that this
theorem cannot settle.
     The loopholes do not weaken the theorem. They strengthen it. They explain why real data shows small
deviations (the assumptions are only approximately satisfied), and where to look for new physics (wherever an
assumption is systematically violated). How often systems in nature satisfy one of these equations in the first place
is a separate empirical question that stability cannot settle.


I.6    The principle as version 3.13 stated it
Superseded statement. Version 3.13 tied a step that multiplies to a power law and a step that adds to an
exponential; Section 5 shows that both the input and the output operation must be named, since a state



                                                          87
                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


multiplied at every tick grows exponentially in the tick count. The registered statement of Law I, the ARC
Principle, a law under test, is the theory registration’s; the wording below is version 3.13’s.
     Many growing systems amplify themselves recursively: each output becomes input for the next step. Under the
stated composition and regularity conditions, the ways the steps can combine give the first two forms below; the
linear and logarithmic cells of the grid are set out in Section 4, and the third case below, a capped process, lies
outside the grid:
      • If each step multiplies the input by a fraction (like blood vessels branching into smaller vessels, each carrying
        a fraction of the flow), the result is a power law.
      • If each step adds independently (like radioactive atoms decaying one by one, or neutrons splitting atoms that
        release more neutrons), the result is an exponential.
      • If growth is capped by a physical limit (like enzyme binding sites filling up), the result is a saturation curve,
        which comes from bounded dynamics rather than from a Cauchy equation.

The ARC Principle. The ARC Principle is Law I of the ARC Theory, a law under test (Section 16): under the
stated composition and regularity conditions, the composition operator, how the steps of recursive amplification
combine, determines the functional form of the resulting scaling law. Multiplicative composition produces power
laws. Additive composition produces exponentials. Bounded composition, which lies outside the Cauchy grid,
produces saturation curves. The grid itself has four cells, the fourth logarithmic, and it does not exhaust scaling
laws (Section 4).


I.7    The network-partition reading of version 3.13
Superseded reading, kept with its sources (Table 8, the rows on the transport exponent, on inventory, mass
and the bound, and on cross-domain comparisons). Version 3.13 set the exponent by the dimension of the
supply network alone; Section 6 shows that demand, route length, speed and a mass bridge must each be
specified. The jellyfish value of 0.94 was not verified at source (Appendix F.5). Its readings of a growth
front, a percolation cluster, a crack network and a fault surface as networks of a given dimension are
interpretations, not derivations; the source status of each physics row, and the earthquake relation as a
conditional change of variables, are in Appendix F. Its domain statement is restated with its present scope
in Appendix D.1.
     No aggregate error is stated. The 2.5 per cent printed until this version included the two cosmology rows at
0.0 per cent, which this paper grades as analytic identities, and it is withdrawn, for the same reason as the physics
aggregate after Table 3; each row keeps its own comparison and source.
     A critical clarification: the formula predicts the scaling exponent for organisms whose metabolism is limited by
the dimensionality of their internal transport network: what resource transport network (RTN) theory calls the
supply network. This is the dimension of the network that delivers resources to tissue, not the dimension of the body
shape. A mammal has a three-dimensional circulatory system; 𝑑 = 3. A filamentous fungus has one-dimensional
cytoplasmic streaming through its hyphae; 𝑑 = 1 (not rejected by published fungal data, a mean of three factors on
different axes, with no equivalence shown; Section 14).

† Note on 𝑑 = 2 in biology.     No known organism possesses a genuinely two-dimensional hierarchical space-filling
transport network. Jellyfish and other cnidarians have gastrovascular canals, but these are not hierarchical and
are not the primary route for oxygen delivery (oxygen enters by diffusion through thin tissue layers). Published
metabolic scaling data for scyphomedusae shows near-isometric scaling (exponent approximately 0.94; Purcell
2009), not 2/3. Colonial bryozoans with 2D sheet growth also scale isometrically (Hartikainen et al. 2014). The
𝑑 = 2 prediction is therefore untested in biology; not because it has failed, but because the required organism has
not been identified. In cosmology the value 2/3 appears as an identity (the Friedmann matter-era solution under the
change of variables), which carries no evidence; in physics it appears in the percolation and fragmentation rows,
whose grades Section 6.1 gives. This defines a boundary of the formula’s biological applicability.
    Organisms that exchange gases primarily through their body surface (like flatworms breathing through their
integument) are limited by surface area geometry rather than internal transport network geometry. Surface area



                                                           88
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


(SA) theory predicts different exponents from RTN theory for these organisms. Thommen et al. (2019) report
Kleiber’s three-quarter scaling in the planarian Schmidtea mediterranea, which lacks a vascular network and
distributes metabolic energy through the branched tubes of its intestine; they trace the scaling not to a fall in each
cell’s metabolic rate with size but to a size-dependent increase in mass per cell, caused by energy storage. A
three-quarter exponent can therefore arise without a three-dimensional vascular network; this paper’s reading of 3/4
as a network-partition value is one account of it, not the only one. Flatworms lie outside the formula’s domain:
𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) applies where metabolism is limited by internal transport, not external exchange.
     On this reading, mammals scale as the 34 power because their vascular networks are three-dimensional: 𝑑 = 3,
so 𝛼 = 3/(3 + 1) = 3/4.
     The 𝑑 = 2 prediction (𝛼 = 2/(2 + 1) = 2/3) applies to any system with a genuinely two-dimensional hierarchical
transport network. In biology, no organism with a validated 2D hierarchical network has yet been identified (see
note above).
     The formula does not care whether the system is a whale or a fungus. It cares only about the dimension of the
hierarchical transport network.
     For every system in the class the formula covers, one whose growth is carried through a space-filling hierarchical
network, 𝑑 is finite (1, 2 or 3), and so 𝛼 is always less than 1: such a system suffers diminishing returns. Systems
outside that class can exceed one (Section 10). How capability grows with recursive depth is a different pair of
variables, which the geometry of a network does not fix (Section 11).

Beyond biology: the network-partition identity. In the theory’s three-tier domain statement this paper
works the composed tier: the mathematics of form reaches everything that composes, stars included, and it explains
the shape of their scaling laws, never their raising; Laws I to III themselves govern only what runs under a corrector.
Section 16 gives each law’s status.
     The biological comparisons of Table 2 are striking, but the formula is not restricted to living things. The same
equation, 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1), appears wherever a 𝑑-dimensional hierarchical network partitions a (𝑑 + 1)-dimensional
space. Four physics rows are catalogued in Table 3; two of them (KPZ in one dimension and two-dimensional
percolation) are analytic identities, and the earthquake relation appears separately as a variable-pair case study,
𝐵 = 2𝑏/3 at 𝑏 = 1 being a definitional identity (Appendix A.2 records the change from the earlier table):

    Mean absolute error across all physics predictions: WITHDRAWN. The previously reported below-0.2%
aggregate is retracted, not adjusted: with the KPZ 1D and 2D percolation rows retiered as analytic identities and the



                                                          89
                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


earthquake row deleted as a definitional identity (as set out in this section), a mean over the remaining rows would be
a mean over analytic values and unsourced reference values, which is small by construction and does not represent
agreement with nature. A row-by-row provenance audit is required before any replacement aggregate is stated.
    These are not biological systems. They are rocks, growing crystal surfaces, and phase transitions (earthquakes
appear separately below as a variable-pair case study rather than as a table row). On this paper’s reading, each
physical system contains a network of the appropriate dimension:
      • KPZ surface growth (Kardar et al. 1986) in one spatial dimension: the growing front is a 1D network
        partitioning 2D space. The roughness exponent is exactly 12 by derivation (an exact value; no derivation here
        links it to 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1)).
      • Percolation on a two-dimensional lattice: the percolation cluster is a 2D fractal network. Through the
        hyperscaling relation 2 − 𝛼 = 𝑑𝜈, with 𝜈 = 4/3, the specific-heat exponent is 𝛼 = −2/3, so the specific-heat
        analogue scales with the exponent +2/3 (Table 3).
      • Brittle fragmentation: cracks propagate as a branching network through the material. Earlier versions of
        this paper printed 23 in two dimensions and 34 in three without a source; the values await the provenance audit
        (Section 17).
      • Earthquake energy (variable-pair case study, not a table row): seismic ruptures propagate along two-
        dimensional fault surfaces. The Gutenberg-Richter law describes how earthquake frequency scales with
        magnitude; its 𝐵-value (linking energy release to frequency) is derived analytically as 𝐵 = 2𝑏/3, which
        at 𝑏 = 1 evaluates to ≈ 2/3 on both sides and is therefore a definitional identity rather than an empirical
        confirmation. The catalogue 𝑏-value varies by region and estimator (roughly 0.8 to 1.2, a range this paper
        gives without a source), so 𝐵 = 2𝑏/3 ranges roughly 0.53 to 0.80 and straddles 12 , 23 and 34 , which cannot
        discriminate between 𝑑 = 1, 𝑑 = 2 and 𝑑 = 3. Gutenberg-Richter is retained here as the sharpest variable-pair
        case in the corpus: earthquake magnitude is defined as a logarithm of released energy, so magnitude against
        energy is multiplicative input with additive output and belongs in the logarithmic cell as a coordinate map,
        whereas the frequency-magnitude relation itself is exponential and the frequency-energy relation is a power
        law. Naming the variable pair at each site is the remedy for the apparent cross-paper contradiction between
        Paper VII’s additive-and-exponential recording of Gutenberg-Richter and this paper’s earlier 𝑑 = 2 power-law
        recording.
    The formula also has clear failures. It does not predict the Ising model critical exponents, polymer scaling
(Flory exponent), or galaxy clustering correlations. The common feature is that these systems lack space-filling
hierarchical networks.

Domain of applicability. 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) applies wherever a 𝑑-dimensional hierarchical network optimally
partitions a (𝑑 + 1)-dimensional space: from the branching arteries inside a whale to the fracture networks inside a
shattered rock. It is a network-partition identity, not a universal scaling law. Its failures are as informative as its
successes: systems without hierarchical space-filling networks (such as magnets near their critical temperature,
chain molecules, and galaxy clusters) do not follow this formula, consistent with the theory’s prediction.


I.8    The opening of Section 8 of version 3.13
Kept with a correction of scope. A fission’s neutron yield is an average, and whether each neutron causes
another fission depends on the medium and the geometry; Section 8 states the branching description with
an effective multiplication factor for that reason.
     𝐸 = 𝑚𝑐2 (Einstein 1905) gives the energy of a mass at rest. A nuclear reaction releases only the difference in
binding energy between what goes in and what comes out, a small fraction of the rest energy, and nuclear energy is
powerful because nuclear binding energies are about a million times larger than chemical ones. The grid classifies
the chain reaction’s exponential growth; it does not explain it.
     But 𝐸 = 𝑚𝑐2 alone does not explain the atomic bomb. It tells you how much energy is in each atom. It does
not explain why the energy release is so catastrophically rapid.
     The chain reaction explains that. When a uranium-235 atom splits, it releases 2 to 3 neutrons. Each neutron can
split another atom. Each generation multiplies the number of fission events by a factor 𝑘:



                                                          90
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


I.9    The geometric bound as version 3.13 stated it
Superseded scope (Table 8, the row on inventory, mass and the bound). Version 3.13 read the bound as
a property of physical space for every network of its class; Proposition 3 now states it for the declared
throughput-cost pair under a demand-distance premise. The two counterexamples it names are kept in
Section 10.
     The only way to reach 𝛼 = 1 is 𝑑 = ∞. An infinite-dimensional network. And the only way to exceed 1 is for
the formula to no longer apply.
     Physical networks (blood vessels, river tributaries, crack patterns, seismic faults) must exist in physical
space. Physical space has three dimensions. You cannot build a four-dimensional branching network inside a
three-dimensional body.

The geometric bound. For a system whose growth is carried through a space-filling hierarchical network
of finite dimension, the exponent stays below one, because 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) is below one for every finite 𝑑 (the earlier
derivations’ result). Systems outside that class can exceed one: prokaryote metabolic rate scales superlinearly with
mass (DeLong et al. 2010), and quantities reflecting wealth creation and innovation in cities scale superlinearly
with population (Bettencourt et al. 2007). Within the class, the reason is not friction. Not heat dissipation. Not
energy loss of any kind. It is that physical space has a finite number of dimensions, and a network embedded in
finite-dimensional space has a finite effective dimension, and 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1 for all finite 𝑑.
     The constraint is geometric, not thermodynamic. No amount of engineering, no level of technology, can
overcome it. You can make a network more efficient, but you cannot make three-dimensional space have four
dimensions. The geometric scaling bound is the shape of space itself.
     That 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1 for every finite 𝑑 is a mathematical necessity, not an empirical observation, and it follows
directly from the formula; whether a given system belongs to the class is an empirical question, which no written
rule yet decides (Section 17).


I.10    The escape and the quantum-error-correction regimes of version 3.13
Superseded readings, kept as dated (Table 8, the rows on recursive gain and phase claims and on correction
axes). The new dimension that each layer of self-reference creates is a figure of speech, not a measured
quantity; capability against depth needs its own model (Section 11). Version 3.13 classed the correction
cycle as bounded composition; a constant per-cycle error does not bound cumulative failure, and code
distance, qubit count and elapsed cycles are different inputs (Section 11.4). The surface-code preprint was
public from 24 August 2024 and the article appeared on 9 December 2024; its relation to this programme
is convergent timing, not a prediction. The sentences on Law III and the ARC Bound are version 3.13’s;
the theory registration and Section 11 give their present form, and Section 16 gives Unit 82’s status.
     A brain is physical: its blood vessels branch through three-dimensional space, paying the geometric tax that
every network of that class pays.
     The computation a brain or a machine performs is physical too, and obeys every physical bound. What differs
is the variable. The bound 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) concerns how metabolic rate grows with the mass of a body supplied through
a space-filling network; the ARC Principle concerns how capability grows with recursive depth. Nothing in the
geometry of the network fixes the second exponent, which is why it has to be measured, and why a value above one
would contradict no physical law.
     Each layer of recursive self-reference creates, in effect, a new dimension. The ‘network’ of recursive thought is
not constrained by the skull’s geometry. The skull constrains the hardware. It does not constrain the software.

Quantum error correction: three regimes classified. Quantum computing provides a striking illustration
of ARC composition operators working within a single technology.
    A quantum computer’s raw error rate is a physical process: each qubit decoheres through interaction with its
environment. Independent errors at a fixed rate per operation make an error-free run exponentially unlikely as
circuits deepen. Left uncorrected, this is Pattern 2, and it makes useful quantum computation impossible beyond
roughly the inverse of the error rate per operation.


                                                         91
                                 The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


     Quantum error correction changes the composition operator. It takes the output of each layer of computation
and feeds it back through a recursive correction cycle: measure syndromes, identify errors, apply corrections, and
feed the corrected state forward as input to the next cycle. This is recursive amplification with bounded composition:
below threshold, each correction cycle holds the logical error per cycle at a fixed level for a given code distance,
instead of letting it compound with depth. On this paper’s reading that is Pattern 3, saturation, which lies outside
the Cauchy grid.
     Below threshold, the surface code suppresses the logical error rate exponentially in code distance, by a factor
Λ = 2.14 ± 0.02 for each increase of two (Google Quantum AI and Collaborators 2025); the preprint was public
from 24 August 2024 and the article appeared in Nature on 9 December 2024. Code distance adds and the logical
error multiplies, so this regime sits in the grid’s exponential cell.

                                   Table 22. Quantum error correction’s regimes.

threshold                 distance                                                       AI and Collaborators 2025)

     One technology. Three regimes, each with its scaling form; the framework classifies two of them into cells of
the grid and the third as bounded composition, outside it. This is precisely analogous to the nuclear fission example
(Section 8): raw decoherence, quantum-error-correction cycles and logical-error suppression were each characterised
independently in the quantum-computing literature, so the framework classifies them as additive composition,
bounded composition and, for the logical error, additive composition in code distance rather than deriving them
from first principles. Classification is a correspondence of structure with those independent characterisations, not
empirical confirmation of the Cauchy grid; the paragraph ‘What the grid does and does not explain’ in Section 8
records the same distinction for the chain reaction.

The structural connection. The full development of the intelligence scaling result, including the ARC Bound
(𝛼 ≤ 2; the conjectured value of Law III, the ARC Ceiling, a law under test, at 𝛾 = 1/2; a scaling limit not a speed
limit, the most a recursively self-correcting system can grow and still remain correctable; systems can exceed it, but
they do not remain stably self-correctable, and transient excursions are the predicted supercritical regime rather than
a refutation; a durably stable 𝛼 > 2 refutes it; Paper X retires the capability-growth law as a binding law, retaining
the 𝛽 > 𝑘 co-scaling condition), the Eden Protocol for safe recursive scaling, and a blinded cross-architecture
replication on large language models (Paper II), is presented in companion papers. Here we note only the structural
implication: the same principle that explains why a whale’s heart beats slowly also bears on how intelligence
scales with recursive depth.

Law III, the ARC Ceiling, a law under test. The stability ceiling is the reciprocal of one minus the corrector’s
own scaling exponent 𝛾. The value 𝛾 = 1/2 (independence-accumulation) is what returns the ceiling of 2 the bound
records; that value is on trial: the theory registration carries the inequality, that the ceiling is at most two (P6 with
P20), and Unit 82, registered on OSF on 27 October 2026 and not yet run (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/JUFG4), tests the
premise beneath the value two (P17). The law owns the ground; the ceiling stands on it. The form is settled; its

depth is not: the relation is derived inside a pacing model, not from a joint model of capability growth, correction
accumulation, error covariance, correction delay and absolute harm, so it stands as a minimal-model result rather

one commitment in two notations, and neither is evidence for the other. Equation (14) has the same form as this
