I.11    Evidence passages of version 3.13
Superseded analyses and summaries (Table 8, the rows on biological comparisons, cross-domain
comparisons and contribution and priority). The derivations from different physics keep their attribution;
that they are complementary instances of one set of constraints, and that those constraints suffice, is not
established (Section 2.3), and the novelty sentence among them was made without a dated literature search
(Appendix E). The level entries keep version 3.13’s grading; its identities carry no evidence (Section 9).
The companion table gives each paper’s status on 31 October 2026; Appendix D.3 names the companions
now.

Derivations from different physics, one form. The most striking feature of the 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) formula is that
it has been derived from radically different physics. West, Brown and Enquist (1997; 1999) derived it from the
geometry of hierarchical, space-filling fractal vascular networks. Separately, Demetrius (2003; 2006) and Demetrius
& Tuszynski (2010) derived the same formula from quantum statistical mechanics, applying the Debye model of
thermal properties to coupled energy-transducing oscillator networks embedded in 𝑑-dimensional space. Different
physics. Different starting assumptions. The same equation. The same predictions: 𝑑 = 1 gives 12 , 𝑑 = 2 gives 23 ,
𝑑 = 3 gives 34 , and 𝑑 → ∞ gives 1.
     The field has treated these as competing theories (reviewed in Agutter & Tuszynski 2011). This paper’s reading
is that they are not competing but complementary: both are physical instantiations of the same set of mathematical
constraints. Cauchy’s multiplicative functional equation (1821) constrains any system with continuous multiplicative
composition to the power-law family. The 𝑑-dimensional space-filling condition, combined with a conservation
or optimisation constraint on resource flow, then constrains the exponent within that family to 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1). West’s
branching networks satisfy multiplicative composition (branching ratios), space-filling, and energy minimisation.
Demetrius’s oscillator chains satisfy multiplicative composition (Debye functions), 𝑑-dimensional embedding, and
steady-state energy balance. Both satisfy the three conditions. If those conditions suffice, both must arrive at the
same formula and the convergence is not coincidental; whether they suffice is the open problem of Section 2.3.
     To our knowledge, this explanation for why the two derivations converge has not been previously published.
The observation that Cauchy’s theorem, combined with space-filling and conservation, provides the meta-constraint
underlying both programmes appears to be novel; no dated literature search has been recorded for it (Appendix E).

The three values. The earlier derivations already give 12 , 23 and 34 from the same formula (Section 2). Three
numbers, for one-, two- and three-dimensional transport networks. One equation with no fitted parameter: 𝑑 is
set by the network’s dimension. The values at 𝑑 = 1 (filamentous fungi) and 𝑑 = 3 (vertebrates and invertebrates
with three-dimensional transport) are compared with published measurements in Level 2a; the value at 𝑑 = 2 is
predicted and untested in biology. The 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) formula is derived, in its general form or as a special case, in
the earlier work that Section 2.1 lists, each given what it states. The contribution of this paper is not the formula
itself but the unifying Cauchy framework that explains why these derivations converge on the same form: Cauchy’s
multiplicative functional equation constrains continuous multiplicative composition to the power-law family, and
the space-filling condition combined with a conservation or optimisation constraint on resource flow would select
the exponent 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) within that family. If those conditions suffice, the convergence is compelled; whether they
do is open (Section 2.3).

Note on the 𝑑 = 2 biological prediction. The note on 𝑑 = 2 in Section 6 applies here: no known organism
has a genuinely two-dimensional hierarchical transport network, so the 𝑑 = 2 value is untested in biology. Published
data show near-isometric scaling for jellyfish and colonial bryozoans (Purcell 2009; Hartikainen et al. 2014;


                                                         93
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


Glazier 2006), not 2/3; planarians show Kleiber’s three-quarter scaling, which Thommen et al. (2019) trace to
body-size-dependent energy storage (Section 6).

Level 2b: physics rows. The same formula gives the values of four physics rows (Section 6.1): two are
analytic identities, which carry no evidence, and the two fragmentation rows await sourced values. Mean error:
WITHDRAWN. The previously reported below-0.2% aggregate is retracted, not adjusted, for the reason Section 6.1
gives. The formula fails where systems lack hierarchical space-filling networks (Ising model, polymer scaling,
galaxy correlations), defining its domain of applicability.

Level 2c: the heart-rate chain. The chain 𝑑 = 3 → 𝛼 = 3/4 → 𝑀 −1/4 agrees with the mouse-to-elephant
comparison of Section 7, 7 per cent off; the 𝑀 −1/4 heart-rate allometry itself is classical (Stahl 1967).

Level 4: structural identities (no evidence). The Friedmann equation is algebraically identical to the ARC
formula under 𝑑 = 2/(1 + 3𝑤). The cosmic boundary (𝑤 = −1/3) maps to 𝑑 → ∞, where 𝛼 → 1. Under that
change of variables the image of one is the other, so the agreement is a property of the map and not a second
derivation (Section 9).

Level 5: the geometric bound. The proof that 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1 for all finite 𝑑 is a mathematical deduction, not
an empirical finding. It requires no data and cannot be falsified by experiment.

Level 6: open predictions. The 𝑑 = 1 fungal data (now included in Level 2a) does not reject the prediction of
0.500 (a mean of three factors on different axes; no equivalence shown) but is based on colony-level measurements
with narrow mass ranges; definitive confirmation requires individual-hypha respirometry (Section 14). The neural
scaling prediction, Prediction 2, is withdrawn; Section 14 sets out the published measurements that refute it. The
geometric scaling bound prediction (no physical system with multiplicative composition through a hierarchical
network will ever have 𝛼 ≥ 1) remains untested. However, the Cauchy-level predictions, that recursive composition
in each domain must follow the operator class dictated by the composition structure, have now been compared in
Paper VII across 25 empirical domains (50-domain tiered suite), with 19 of 25 preferring the predicted family under
the March 2026 rule (Level 3).

I.13    The prediction record of version 3.13: data, limitations and closing statements
Dated record. The three fungal fits the first paragraph introduces are in Table 14, each with its own variable
pair; their mean and pooled reading are withdrawn (Table 8, the row on biological comparisons). The
book’s names for the loops are the author’s own, given in his book; the measurements use the operational
names. The closing sentences state how version 3.13 asked its predictions to be read; Section 14 gives
their present dispositions beside their printed wording.
     Preliminary data. Aguilar-Trigueros et al. (2017, ISME Journal 11:2175) compiled published metabolic
scaling measurements for fungi, in what they believe is the first attempt to synthesise such relationships among
groups of fungi:
     The three are colony-level scaling factors at 20 degrees Celsius, the first two against colony biomass and the
third against colony radial extension rate, each with its own standard error; their mean, 0.547, is this paper’s, since
the source gives no mean. All three confidence intervals include the predicted value of 0.500, and all three also
include 2/3, so no single factor separates the two. These results do not reject the 𝑑 = 1 prediction of 0.500, though
they show no equivalence with it, their mean being of three factors on different axes, and are not yet definitive
confirmation of it. Three limitations constrain the evidential weight: these are colony-level measurements (not
individual hyphae), the datasets span narrow mass ranges, and the exponent is temperature-dependent (saprotrophic
fungi at 28°C show 𝛼 = 0.85, with poor statistics: 𝑟 2 = 0.14, 𝑝 = 0.52; Crowther and Bradford 2013). The authors
describe these results as ‘hypothesis generators.’

A note on names. This paper names the Eden Protocol’s self-correction loops by what each checks and how it
was measured: the purpose check, the stakeholder-care check (scored as stakeholder care) and the universalisability
check. The book in which the author first set the loops out, Infinite Architects (Eastwood 2026a), calls them the
Purpose Loop, the Love Loop and the Moral Loop; the paper uses the operational terms because its measurements
score what each check does, and because the values the loops carry are for international consensus and are not
decided here.
    If prediction 1 yields 𝛼 = 0.50 for organisms with genuine 1D internal transport, the formula 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) will
agree with measurement in biology at 𝑑 = 1 and 𝑑 = 3, with 𝑑 = 2 awaiting an organism with a two-dimensional
hierarchical transport network (Section 6).
    If prediction 3 fails, this paper is wrong.


I.14    The status, limitations and conclusion of version 3.13
Superseded summaries. Sections 16, 17 and 18 state the present scope. The conclusion’s sentence on the
comprehensibility of the universe is set out, at its present status, at the end of Section 18.

What this paper does and does not show.

What it shows. As mathematics, under the stated regularity conditions, a function that carries one of the two
structures on the positive reals into one of the two falls into one of four families (Theorem 1); that classification of
homomorphisms does not exhaust scaling laws. The paper offers an account of why derivations of 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1)
from different assumptions converge, the formula and its values being earlier work’s (Section 2), and it compares
the formula with published exponents: not rejected at 𝑑 = 1 for filamentous fungi on colony-level data (a mean
of three factors on different axes; no equivalence shown), in agreement at 𝑑 = 3, on the group means compiled
here, for organisms with three-dimensional transport, untested at 𝑑 = 2 in biology for want of an organism, and set
beside four physics rows, two of them analytic identities and two, of fragmentation, not yet sourced (Sections 6
and 12). For every finite 𝑑 the exponent stays below one in the class of systems whose growth is carried through a
space-filling hierarchical network (Section 10).

What it does not show. It does not show that every scaling law takes one of these forms, or that the three
conditions suffice for 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1), which is the open problem of Section 2.3. The Friedmann mapping and the
coincidence of the two boundaries are identities under a change of variables and carry no evidence (Section 9).



                                                          96
                                The origin of scaling laws in recursive composition


It claims no exception to physics for intelligence: how capability grows with recursive depth is a different pair
of variables, which the geometry does not fix and which has to be measured (Section 11). Its summaries of the
programme’s AI experiments report one model’s inconclusive fit for capability against depth and one unregistered
alignment run under the code-review caveat of Section 12.4, and its Eden Protocol pilot was scored without blinding.
Prediction 2 is withdrawn. No result in this paper is a registered test of any proposition of the theory registration.

Limitations.
    • Scope of the classification. Cauchy’s classification covers homomorphisms between the additive and
      multiplicative structures under regularity. Most systems are not such homomorphisms, and saturation, critical
      exponents, curvature on log axes and multi-regime crossovers lie outside the grid (Section 4).
    • No proof of sufficiency. No formal proof yet shows that multiplicative composition, space filling and
      conservation suffice for 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1); the convergence of the earlier derivations could reflect shared assumptions
      (Section 2.3).
    • The biological comparison. The fungal values are three colony-level scaling factors from small data sets
      over narrow ranges, one of them against colony extension rate rather than biomass, which their authors
      present as hypothesis generators; definitive testing needs respirometry on isolated hyphae (Prediction 1).
      No organism with a genuinely two-dimensional hierarchical transport network has been identified. The
      three-group comparison of Section 12 was computed with a two-dimensional group that the note on 𝑑 = 2
      sets aside, and the aggregate error that Section 6 printed until this version, which counted the two cosmology
      identities, is withdrawn (Appendix A). The four biological values of Table 2 are each their source’s own,
      read at that source; of the seven group exponents behind the three-dimensional mean of Table 10, three (fish,
      amphibians and crustaceans) are not yet confirmed at source in this paper, and published group exponents
      depend on the data set and the method (Section 12).
    • The physics rows. Two of the four rows are analytic identities, which carry no evidence, and the two
      fragmentation rows have no sourced values yet and await the row-by-row provenance audit that Section 6.1
      names.
    • The bound. No written rule yet decides, before an exponent is fitted, which systems are governed by
      multiplicative composition through a finite-dimensional hierarchical network, so a counterexample could be
      reclassified after the fact (Prediction 4).
    • The cosmology. The mapping 𝑑 = 2/(1 + 3𝑤) is an identity; the causal reading of Section 9 is this paper’s
      interpretation, and it conflicts with established cosmology on when and how the cosmic web formed (Cautun
      et al. 2014).
    • The AI-side results. The capability fit is one model’s and inconclusive; the alignment tiers come from one
      unregistered run under the October 2026 code-review caveat; the Eden Protocol pilot was scored without
      blinding (Section 12).
    • Novelty. The paper’s novelty statements were made without a dated literature search (Appendix E).

Conclusion. Under stated conditions, how a recursive process combines its steps fixes the family its growth can
take, among four. Within the power-law family, the exponent 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) that earlier work derived for hierarchical
networks agrees with published values where such a network partitions space, and this paper offers an account
of why those derivations converge. Where the same form appears in cosmology it is an identity. The exponent
of capability against recursive depth is a different quantity, which this geometry does not fix and which has to be
measured; the registered programme exists to measure it.
      “One may say ‘the eternal mystery of the world is its comprehensibility.’ ”
      Albert Einstein (1936)
     [. . . ]
     Every system whose growth is carried through a space-filling hierarchical network of finite dimension stays
below the geometric scaling bound: the scaling exponent 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) approaches 1 but never reaches it, and such
systems suffer diminishing returns. Systems outside that class can exceed one (Section 10).
     Intelligence is no exception: its computation is physical. How its capability grows with recursive depth is a
different pair of variables, which the geometry does not fix and which has to be measured (Section 11).

Glossary.
composition operator how the steps of a recursive process combine: by multiplication, by addition, or under a cap
homomorphism a function that carries one algebraic structure (addition or multiplication on the positive reals)
      into one of the two
regularity continuity, or measurability, or boundedness on an interval, the conditions under which Cauchy’s
      equations have only the named solutions
network-partition identity this paper’s reading of 𝛼 = 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1): it applies where a 𝑑-dimensional hierarchical
      network partitions a (𝑑 + 1)-dimensional space
analytic identity; definitional identity a row whose agreement holds by derivation or by definition, not by
      measurement, or an exact value that equals the formula’s number with no derivation linking the two; such a
      row carries no evidence
variable pair the two quantities a scaling relation connects; naming it decides which cell of the grid a relation sits
      in
Phase I, II, III sublinear, exponential and saturating growth in the paper’s phase diagram
geometric scaling bound that 𝑑/(𝑑 + 1) < 1 for every finite 𝑑, for systems whose growth is carried through a
      space-filling hierarchical network
ARC Bound 𝛼 ≤ 2, the conjectured value of Law III, the ARC Ceiling, at 𝛾 = 1/2
Laws I to V Law I, the ARC Principle; Law II, the ARC Co-Scaling Law; Law III, the ARC Ceiling; Law IV, the
      ARC Persistence Law; Law V, the ARC Embedding Law; each a law under test
purpose check, stakeholder-care check, universalisability check the Eden Protocol’s self-correction loops,
      named by what each checks (Section 14)
preregistered by timestamp; registered a design fixed and timestamped before its data, described with its date
      and receipt; “registered” only for what the author submitted and OSF accepted


I.16      The novelty statements of version 3.13
Dated record. Each statement was made without a dated literature search; Appendix E sets the present
contribution beside the closest earlier work, and Table 8 gives the present position. The analogy with
Newton and Einstein is the earlier edition’s, with its own concession.
     The dated correspondence note of 19 September 2026 (Appendix A.2) records that the paper’s universal novelty
statements were made with no literature search and that no novelty verdict is recorded either way. This appendix
keeps each statement as printed, beside the closest earlier work the paper itself names, with its status in this version.
A dated search remains to be made.

The analogy, moved here from Section 2.2. Newton discovered that gravity exists and measured its effects.
Einstein discovered why: geometry. Mass curves spacetime, and objects follow the curves. Newton was not wrong.
He was incomplete.
     Kleiber found that the basal metabolism of thirteen groups of animals, from ring doves to steers, is best
calculated per 0.74 power of body weight, rather than per unit of body surface as the earlier surface law held. West,
Brown, and Enquist derived the mechanism for three dimensions and gave the exponent for 𝐷 dimensions. This
paper’s principle is the one Section 2.2 states. The likeness to Einstein’s work is limited: Einstein’s mechanism was
compelled to fit a dated observational anomaly (Mercury’s perihelion advance), whereas the four-cell grid here
reorganises earlier derivations that were already correct in their own domains, and the empirical work that would
settle it at law level (Section 11 and Section 16) is not yet run.
